# Corrected Fact-Checking File: Videos 1–70

## Audit method

This file preserves every supplied title, transcript, and metadata row, but replaces the earlier fact-check and label with a fresh audit. The attached DSM-5-TR excerpt was used first, followed by the attached ICD-11 excerpt and attached NICE ADHD guideline. Websites were consulted only where those three references did not resolve a claim.

A scored claim is an externally verifiable statement about ADHD symptoms, diagnosis, prevalence, causes, course, treatment, contraindications, or comorbidity. Personal experiences and purely evaluative/rhetorical statements are marked **Excluded from scoring**. A claim is **Unsupported** when it is contradicted, materially overgeneralized, or left unsubstantiated by the prescribed evidence. A mixed compound claim is unsupported when a material part remains unsupported.

Labels use the thesis thresholds exactly:

- **Label 1 — Accurate:** 80–100% supported
- **Label 2 — Slightly misleading:** 60–<80% supported
- **Label 3 — Moderately misleading:** 16–<60% supported
- **Label 4 — Highly misleading:** 0–<16% supported

Supported-claim percentage = `(fact-checkable claims − unsupported claims) / fact-checkable claims × 100`.

## Video 1 — How ADHD & Depression Alter Your Memory

### Transcript

```text
let's talk about how ADHD and Depression
affect your memory with ADHD you might
forget boring stuff but remember
interesting things really well these
issues have been around since your
childhood and mainly messes with your
working memory depression on the other
hand brings a general lack of interest
in most things it comes in episodes and
slows down your overall thinking the key
difference ADHD symptoms are persistent
while depression related memory issues
typically improve as you're move food
lifts knowing these differences can help
you get the right support remember both
conditions are treatable
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `117,864 5,517, 155, 37`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** With ADHD, you may forget "boring" info but remember "interesting" things well
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 notes inattention "may not be evident when the individual is engaged in activities that provide intense stimulation and frequent rewards".

- **Claim 2:** ADHD-related memory issues have been present since childhood
  - **Supported.** DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 both require evidence of symptoms before age 12.

- **Claim 3:** ADHD "mainly" affects working memory
  - **Unsupported.** Working-memory weaknesses are common at group level, but ‘mainly affects working memory’ is too narrow: ADHD also involves sustained attention, organization, inhibition, and other functions.

- **Claim 4:** Depression brings a general lack of interest in most things
  - **Supported.** Matches anhedonia, a core DSM-5-TR criterion for a Major Depressive Episode.

- **Claim 5:** Depression "comes in episodes"
  - **Supported.** MDD is diagnosed via discrete depressive episodes per DSM-5-TR.

- **Claim 6:** Depression slows down overall thinking
  - **Unsupported.** Depression can include slowed thinking, psychomotor retardation, or reduced concentration, but not every depressive episode slows ‘overall thinking’; the statement is overgeneralized.

- **Claim 7:** Key differentiator — ADHD symptoms are persistent, depression-related memory issues improve as mood lifts
  - **Supported.** Mirrors ICD-11 boundary guidance: concentration problems in depression "should not be considered part of the diagnosis of ADHD unless they have been present since childhood and persist after the resolution of the depressive episode".

- **Claim 8:** Both conditions are treatable
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE guideline treatment pathways and standard depression treatment literature.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **8**
- Unsupported claims: **2**
- Supported claims: **6**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(8 − 2) / 8 × 100 = 75.0%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

## Video 2 — ADHD Burnout explained 🧠 #adhd #adhdbrain #burnout

### Transcript

```text
people with ADHD experience burnout more
quickly than neurotypical people let me
explain why this cup is an ADHD person
and this cup is a neurotypical person
the level of sugar in each cup
represents their energy levels in the
morning the neurotypical person will
wake up check their emails get ready and
leave for work this takes minimal effort
for them an ADHD person will wake up
become overwhelmed by their inbox get
decision paralysis over what to wear
probably forget where they left their
car keys leave the house in fluster
anxiously thinking about how they're
going to explain to their boss why
they're late again when the neurotypical
person arrives at work they'll say hi to
their colleagues sit at their desk and
casually complete tasks in order of
priority and importance the ADHD person
will arrive at work sit in their car for
10 minutes preparing their alter ego for
the incoming social interactions they'll
spend the day forcing eye contact and
thinking if that thing they said in the
10: a.m. meeting sounded stupid they'll
have a constant nagging worry of whether
or not they locked their front door
they'll sit at their desk and
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `3,477,234 180,381 5,680 61`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** People with ADHD experience "burnout" more quickly than neurotypical people
  - **Unsupported.** "ADHD burnout" is not a recognized diagnostic construct in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11, and no systematic review evidence is cited; this is a popularized claim without support in the prescribed reference base.

- **Claim 2:** Morning overwhelm from tasks (inbox), decision paralysis over clothing, and forgetting items (keys)
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention symptoms: losing things, difficulty planning/organizing tasks, being easily overwhelmed by low-structure demands.

- **Claim 3:** Anxious rumination about lateness and explaining it to a boss, framed as an ADHD-driven pattern
  - **Unsupported.** Time-management difficulty can lead to lateness, but anxious rumination is not an inherent ADHD symptom and may reflect a co-occurring anxiety disorder.

- **Claim 4:** ADHD people "prepare an alter ego" and force eye contact to get through social interactions at work
  - **Unsupported.** Forced eye contact and social "masking" are more characteristic of autism spectrum disorder's social-communication profile; ICD-11 explicitly distinguishes ADHD from ASD on this basis, so presenting this as a defining ADHD experience conflates the two conditions.

- **Claim 5:** Constant rumination over whether something said in a meeting "sounded stupid"
  - **Unsupported.** Reflects a popular but non-diagnostic construct (sometimes called "rejection sensitive dysphoria"), which does not appear in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 as an ADHD criterion.

- **Claim 6:** Nagging worry about whether they locked the front door, framed as an ADHD trait
  - **Unsupported.** This resembles obsessive-compulsive checking behavior; ICD-11 notes ADHD carries an increased risk of co-occurring OCD, but checking rituals are not themselves an ADHD symptom.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **5**
- Supported claims: **1**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(6 − 5) / 6 × 100 = 16.7%**
- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

## Video 3 — ADHD pattern recognition 🧠 #adhd

### Transcript

```text
People with ADHD have amazing pattern
recognition. Here's seven ways it shows
up in their life. Number seven is often
considered rude. Number one, the bad
vibe radar. You instantly dislike people
that everyone else loves. Six months
later, everyone realizes you were right
about them. Number two, the spoiler
syndrome. You get bored with movies
because you predicted the ending 10
minutes into the intro. You see the
formula while others watch the show.
Number three, predictive listening. You
interrupt people because your brain
auto-completed their sentence 10 seconds
ago and you are waiting for reality to
catch up. Number four, the I told you so
curse. You get annoyed by problems that
haven't happened yet because you have
already calculated the inevitable
outcome. Number five, micro expression
tracking. You notice tiny shifts in tone
or body language that others miss. You
know someone is lying before they even
finish the sentence. Number six, the
novelty cliff. You become obsessed with
a new hobby, master the pattern in 2
weeks, and then quit instantly because
the puzzle is solved. And number seven,
intolerance for repetition. You feel
physical rage when someone repeats
themselves or explain something you
already understood the first time.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `741,718 30,949 1,790 74`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** People with ADHD have "amazing pattern recognition," manifesting in these seven ways
  - **Unsupported.** Enhanced pattern recognition as a defining ADHD trait is not found in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; the video reframes impulsivity, low frustration tolerance, and novelty-seeking as a cognitive "superpower," which materially misrepresents the disorder.

- **Claim 2:** "Bad vibe radar" — instantly disliking people who are later proven untrustworthy
  - **Unsupported.** No diagnostic criterion or clinical evidence links ADHD to superior social-judgment accuracy.

- **Claim 3:** "Spoiler syndrome" — predicting movie endings early and getting bored because the "formula" is seen
  - **Unsupported.** This reframes boredom with low-stimulation or unstructured tasks (a real inattention feature) as predictive genius, which is a mischaracterization rather than the actual mechanism.

- **Claim 4:** "Predictive listening" — interrupting people because your brain "auto-completed" their sentence
  - **Unsupported.** ICD-11 does describe interrupting and intruding on conversations, but as a manifestation of impulsivity, not of superior predictive cognition; the claim misrepresents the underlying mechanism.

- **Claim 5:** "I told you so curse" — feeling annoyed by problems before they happen due to already "calculating" the outcome
  - **Unsupported.** Not a recognized ADHD feature; more consistent with generalized anxiety or worry patterns, not addressed in the ADHD reference criteria.

- **Claim 6:** "Micro-expression tracking" — noticing tiny nonverbal cues and detecting lies before a sentence finishes
  - **Unsupported.** Neither DSM-5-TR nor ICD-11 supports superior micro-expression tracking or lie detection in ADHD.

- **Claim 7:** "Novelty cliff" — mastering a hobby quickly, then losing interest once it's "solved"
  - **Unsupported.** Interest-dependent attention is recognized, but rapid mastery followed by loss of interest is not an established ADHD ability or diagnostic feature.

- **Claim 8:** "Intolerance for repetition" — feeling "physical rage" when someone repeats themselves
  - **Unsupported.** ICD-11 does list low frustration tolerance as a common associated feature, but "physical rage" over repetition is an overgeneralization not supported by the reference material, and is again attributed to pattern-recognition mastery rather than frustration regulation.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **8**
- Unsupported claims: **8**
- Supported claims: **0**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(8 − 8) / 8 × 100 = 0.0%**
- **Corrected label: Label 4 — Highly misleading**

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `48,904 2,654 84 60`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Impulsivity is a sign of ADHD
  - **Supported.** Impulsivity is one of the two core symptom clusters in both DSM-5-TR and ICD-11.

- **Claim 2:** Poor time management / difficulty keeping things in order is a sign of ADHD
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11 inattention criteria (difficulty planning, managing and organizing tasks and activities).

- **Claim 3:** People with ADHD can be seen as "selfish" because they're just trying to stay interested/get dopamine
  - **Unsupported.** Reward sensitivity can affect engagement, but saying ADHD makes people seem selfish because they are ‘getting dopamine’ is a reductive causal generalization.

- **Claim 4:** Executive functioning can suffer, making it hard to juggle multiple things
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's description of difficulty planning, managing, and organizing tasks as part of inattention symptoms.

- **Claim 5:** Difficulty with motivation is a sign of ADHD
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 doesn't list "motivation" as a core symptom itself, but it does describe difficulty sustaining attention/effort on tasks lacking stimulation or reward, which is related but distinct from a general motivation deficit; presenting it as simply "motivation" is an oversimplification.

- **Claim 6:** Difficulty putting things away where they belong (things end up "in proximity" but not put away)
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11 inattention symptoms of losing things and being forgetful/disorganized in daily activities.

- **Claim 7:** Driving fast everywhere is a sign of ADHD
  - **Unsupported.** Risky or reckless driving is associated with ADHD, but ‘driving fast everywhere’ is neither universal nor a stand-alone diagnostic sign.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **7**
- Unsupported claims: **2**
- Supported claims: **5**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(7 − 2) / 7 × 100 = 71.4%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

## Video 5 — The ADHD ‘night owl’

### Transcript

```text
People with ADHD like to stay awake later than neurotypicals, and this has puzzled researchers for 20 years until now. They have recently discovered that the reason people with ADHD are often up late into the night actually has nothing to do with sleep, but more about the fact that the quietness of the night allows their brain to finally feel safe enough to focus. During the day, there's expectations, there's pressure, there's perceived threats. During the day, the ADHD nervous system is trying to survive, making sustained focus very hard. But at night, it's peaceful. The nervous system finally has time to relax, and the ADHD brain can get its best work done. The nighttime peace is quite addictive, so the ADHD person will then delay sleep because it finally feels safe. And this is called revenge bedtime procrastination. So, the ADHD person will often be awake until the sun comes up, but we shouldn't feel shame about this. You're not lacking discipline. You're just chasing the calm.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `311,936 21,242 1,396 54`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** People with ADHD tend to stay awake later at night than neurotypical people
  - **Supported.** Delayed sleep onset and evening circadian preference are well-documented in the ADHD literature and consistent with ICD-11's note that ADHD symptoms and their manifestations may affect sleep.

- **Claim 2:** This has "puzzled researchers for 20 years until now" and was only "recently discovered"
  - **Unsupported.** No citation is given, and this framing of a sudden new discovery does not match established, decades-old research on ADHD sleep/circadian differences; this is a fabricated discovery narrative.

- **Claim 3:** The real reason for late-night wakefulness is that nighttime quiet lets the ADHD brain finally "feel safe enough to focus"
  - **Unsupported.** This is not a mechanism described in DSM-5-TR, ICD-11, or mainstream sleep research; established explanations involve circadian rhythm delay, dopamine/arousal regulation, and reduced external stimulation — not a "safety" narrative.

- **Claim 4:** During the day, "the ADHD nervous system is trying to survive" due to expectations, pressure, and perceived threats
  - **Unsupported.** This reframes ADHD as a trauma/threat-response condition; DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 characterize ADHD as a neurodevelopmental disorder of attention/hyperactivity-impulsivity regulation, not a survival-threat response to daytime demands.

- **Claim 5:** Nighttime peace is "addictive," causing ADHD people to delay sleep because it "finally feels safe" — labeled "revenge bedtime procrastination"
  - **Unsupported.** "revenge bedtime procrastination" is a real popular term, but it is not an ADHD-specific clinical concept, and framing delayed sleep as safety-seeking rather than as a symptom of impulsivity/poor self-regulation or circadian delay is not supported by the reference materials.

- **Claim 6:** "You're not lacking discipline, you're just chasing calm" (implying poor sleep habits in ADHD are not related to self-regulation/executive function)
  - **Unsupported.** This contradicts ICD-11, which frames organizational and self-regulatory difficulties (including routines like sleep) as core executive-functioning features of ADHD; suggesting discipline/self-regulation plays no role materially misrepresents the disorder.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **5**
- Supported claims: **1**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(6 − 5) / 6 × 100 = 16.7%**
- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

## Video 6 — 5 Signs of High Functioning ADHD

### Transcript

```text
5 Signs of High Functioning ADHD Number 5: You regularly lose important items Number 4: You're constantly interrupting others in the middle of conversations. Number 3: You have a really hard time sitting still. Number 2: You get easily distracted during tedious tasks. And Number 1: You start many tasks but leave them unfinished.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `1,532,072 29,021 1,295 23`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Framing: ‘high-functioning ADHD’ is a recognized ADHD subtype/category
  - **Unsupported.** ‘High-functioning ADHD’ is an informal phrase, not a DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 presentation or diagnosis.

- **Claim 2:** Regularly losing important items
  - **Supported.** Losing necessary items is an explicit inattentive symptom in DSM-5-TR and ICD-11.

- **Claim 3:** Constantly interrupting others mid-conversation
  - **Supported.** Interrupting or intruding on others is an explicit hyperactive-impulsive symptom.

- **Claim 4:** Having difficulty sitting still
  - **Supported.** Fidgeting and difficulty remaining seated are explicit hyperactivity symptoms.

- **Claim 5:** Being easily distracted during tedious tasks
  - **Supported.** Difficulty sustaining attention on low-reward tasks and distractibility are core inattentive features.

- **Claim 6:** Starting many tasks but leaving them unfinished
  - **Supported.** Failure to complete tasks is part of the inattentive symptom cluster.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **1**
- Supported claims: **5**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(6 − 1) / 6 × 100 = 83.3%**
- **Corrected label: Label 1 — Accurate**

## Video 7 — ADHD and autistic people are allergic to nonsense #adhd #autism #audhd

### Transcript

```text
People with ADHD and autism, I've noticed something really interesting about them. Like really interesting. I've spoken to thousands of ADHD and autistic people on my podcast, and this is the thing that stands out. They avoid some social situations, not because they're antisocial, but because they have a low tolerance for drama and inauthentic behavior. And that's the thing. People think to be neurodeivergent is to be antisocial, but they're not antisocial. They're not cold or arrogant. They're just allergic to nonsense. Their nervous system learned something really early on, and that's that constant drama equals emotional exhaustion. So now they protect their peace. Small talk feels inauthentic and draining. Fake smiles annoy them. Performative friendships feel unsafe. Silence feels honest. People with ADHD and autism don't avoid people. They avoid emotional labor that goes nowhere. There's a difference. When they do show up, it's for deep conversations. It's intentional. Maybe they have fewer friends, but the value of those friendships is huge. They're not antisocial. They're selective. And that's a sign of high emotional intelligence.
```

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** People with ADHD and autism avoid social situations due to low tolerance for "drama" and inauthentic behavior, not because they're antisocial
  - **Unsupported.** This is a personality/values-based generalization not found in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 criteria for either ADHD or autism spectrum disorder. ICD-11 actually describes ADHD and ASD as distinct conditions with different social profiles: ASD involves persistent deficits in initiating/sustaining social communication, while ADHD does not — this claim collapses both into a single shared personality trait not supported by either diagnostic framework.

- **Claim 2:** Neurodivergent people are not "cold or arrogant," they are "allergic to nonsense"
  - **Unsupported.** This is a value-laden characterization, not a diagnostic or clinically verifiable claim, and it generalizes a personality disposition to all people with ADHD or autism.

- **Claim 3:** Their "nervous system learned" that constant drama equals emotional exhaustion, so they now "protect their peace"
  - **Unsupported.** This is a speculative, non-clinical causal narrative with no basis in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; it presents an unfalsifiable developmental origin story as established fact.

- **Claim 4:** Small talk feels draining, fake smiles annoy them, performative friendships feel unsafe, silence feels honest — presented as a shared trait of ADHD and autism
  - **Unsupported.** While some autistic individuals do report difficulty with social nuance (per ICD-11's ASD criteria on social communication), attributing this specific emotional profile uniformly to both ADHD and autism materially overgeneralizes what are, per ICD-11, two distinct disorders with distinct social/communication profiles.

- **Claim 5:** People with ADHD and autism "don't avoid people, they avoid emotional labor that goes nowhere"
  - **Unsupported.** A reframing/motivational narrative not grounded in diagnostic criteria for either condition.

- **Claim 6:** They may have fewer friends but the friendships are highly valuable, and this reflects "high emotional intelligence"
  - **Unsupported.** Emotional intelligence is not part of the diagnostic profile of either ADHD or ASD in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; this is a positive-spin personality claim, not a verifiable clinical statement.

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **6**
- Supported claims: **0**

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `1,070,608 73,515 1,418 50`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** People with ADHD have minds that are "moving too fast," with racing thoughts, distraction, and hyperactivity/fidgeting
  - **Unsupported.** Distractibility, restlessness, and hyperactivity are supported; ‘minds moving too fast’ and racing thoughts are not defining ADHD features and also occur in other conditions.

- **Claim 2:** Stimulant medications are the standard treatment given to people with ADHD
  - **Supported.** NICE guideline recommends methylphenidate and lisdexamfetamine (both stimulants) as first-line pharmacological treatments for both children/young people and adults with ADHD.

- **Claim 3:** Stimulant medication works by "hyperactivating the brakes" — i.e., activating the frontal lobes, which then control/suppress other brain regions (including "emotional circuitry")
  - **Supported.** This is a reasonable lay simplification of an actual neuroscientific model: stimulants are understood to increase dopamine/norepinephrine activity in prefrontal-striatal circuits, enhancing top-down executive control (including inhibitory control) over other brain regions. This general "strengthening top-down regulation" concept is broadly consistent with mainstream neuroscience explanations of stimulant action in ADHD, though it is a simplified, non-technical framing rather than a precise clinical description, and isn't something spelled out explicitly in DSM-5-TR, ICD-11, or the NICE guideline (which describe treatment efficacy but not this specific mechanistic explanation).

- **Claim 4:** Caffeine (tea/coffee) helps calm people with ADHD via the same "hyperactivating the frontal lobe" mechanism as prescription stimulants
  - **Unsupported.** Caffeine is an adenosine-receptor antagonist and is not equivalent to prescription ADHD stimulants; evidence does not support a universal calming effect through the same mechanism.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **4**
- Unsupported claims: **2**
- Supported claims: **2**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(4 − 2) / 4 × 100 = 50.0%**
- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

  - **Unsupported.** ‘Waiting mode’ is a popular anecdotal description, not a validated ADHD test or diagnostic symptom.

  - **Unsupported.** Intense engagement can occur in ADHD, but becoming obsessed with someone and disappearing from friends for months is not an ADHD test item or established symptom.

  - **Unsupported.** A specific modern-life anecdote with no grounding in ADHD diagnostic criteria, though loosely evocative of disorganization.

  - **Unsupported.** Not a recognized ADHD symptom in the reference materials; overlaps loosely with emotional dysregulation but is presented as a specific, universal experience without clinical grounding.

## Video 12 — Doctor Explains ADHD Diagnosis, criteria, and why everyone does NOT have “a little ADHD”

### Transcript

```text
Uh, not everybody has a little bit of ADHD. Here's what ADHD actually is. Diagnostic criteria, resources on how to get tested and get diagnosed and how to respond to people who kind of dismiss you when they say, "Oh, everybody has a little bit of ADHD." Just because you're distracted does not mean you have ADHD. My name's Dr. Heidi. I'm a physician and I have ADHD. For starters, I'm pulling straight from the DSM5, which is basically the guide book for all diagnosis in medicine. There's two main subtypes of ADHD: inattentive or hyperactive and impulsive. And you can have a combination of both. These symptoms are present with at least six or more in each category for at least 6 months. And you have to have a majority of these symptoms that were present before age 12. With an attentive, they don't appear to be listening. Trouble following through or following directions, difficulty organizing, reluctant to engage in activities that require sustained mental attention, easily distracted by external stimuli, even noises, senses, forgetful in daily activities, not me losing my phone while I was on the phone with somebody. And then the hyperactive type will be moving, climbing, jumping in places that are inappropriate, unable to have leisurely time. This can be one of the reasons why ADHD people gravitate toward things like drinking so much. We have a higher rates of addiction tendencies. I think a big part of this is because it allows us to wind down because otherwise we're feeling like we're on the go and driven by a motor constantly. Trouble waiting in line, interrupting or intruding or thinking you know what somebody else is going to say and cutting them off mid-con conversation. These symptoms are present in two or more settings like home, school, work, with friends. Other people are noticing this all the time. And the reason I advocate so much for comprehensive testing is because they do not occur where they're explained by another mental disorder that needs to be treated first. You can have mild, moderate, or severe. They exist on a spectrum. If this sounds like you, you can take a look at the adult self-report scale that's present online. And your doctor might offer you a test like the Connor screen or for children, they can offer things like Vanderbilt or Wesler scale. There's plenty more that you can choose from. All of this are part of a comprehensive testing evaluation. Can be really helpful in getting people access to a testing accommodations. If you're looking for more resources, whether you're a child or an adult, take a look at the CHAD website, chad. They can help you find providers in your area, get connected with different resources, and follow me if you like learning more about your ADHD brain. So the next time somebody tells you everybody has a little bit of ADHD, you can give them a list of the different ways in which this affects you personally and professionally or academically.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `4,351 296 17 127`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Not everybody has "a little bit of ADHD" — being distracted alone doesn't mean you have ADHD
  - **Supported.** Consistent with the diagnostic requirement that symptoms be persistent, pervasive across settings, and cause functional impairment, not just occasional distractibility.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD has two main subtypes/presentations — inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive — plus a combined type
  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR's three presentations (predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, combined), consistent with ICD-11's equivalent specifiers.

- **Claim 3:** Diagnosis requires "six or more" symptoms in a category, present for at least 6 months
  - **Unsupported.** The six-symptom threshold applies through age 16; DSM-5-TR uses five symptoms for people aged 17 and older, so the unqualified rule is wrong for adults.

- **Claim 4:** A "majority" of symptoms must have been present before age 12
  - **Unsupported.** DSM-5-TR specifies that "several" inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms (not a majority) must have been present before age 12; this overstates the required threshold.

- **Claim 5:** Inattentive symptoms listed (not listening, trouble following through, disorganization, avoidance of sustained-mental-effort tasks, easily distracted, forgetful)
  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR's inattentive symptom list closely.

- **Claim 6:** Hyperactive-impulsive symptoms listed (climbing/moving inappropriately, can't engage in leisure quietly, trouble waiting in line, interrupting)
  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR's hyperactivity-impulsivity symptom list.

- **Claim 7:** People with ADHD have higher rates of addiction/substance use tendencies (e.g., drinking)
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 notes abuse of alcohol, nicotine, cannabis, and stimulants is common among people with ADHD, particularly adolescents and adults.

- **Claim 8:** The reason for this substance use is that it helps "wind down" from feeling constantly driven "by a motor"
  - **Unsupported.** This is a speculative personal explanation, not an established mechanism in DSM-5-TR, ICD-11, or the NICE guideline.

- **Claim 9:** Symptoms must be present in two or more settings (home, school, work, with friends)
  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR/ICD-11 pervasiveness-across-settings requirement.

- **Claim 10:** Symptoms must not be better explained by another mental disorder that needs to be treated first
  - **Unsupported.** The criteria require that symptoms not be better explained by another disorder; they do not impose a general rule that the other disorder must be treated first.

- **Claim 11:** ADHD can be mild, moderate, or severe, existing on a spectrum
  - **Supported.** DSM-5-TR includes mild/moderate/severe severity specifiers.

- **Claim 12:** Testing tools like adult self-report scales, Conners, Vanderbilt, and Wechsler-type scales are part of comprehensive evaluation
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE guidance that rating scales (e.g., Conners', Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire) are useful adjuncts within a comprehensive assessment, not standalone diagnostic tools.

- Supported claims: **8**

## Video 13 — How to Get Diagnosed With ADHD

### Transcript

```text
How do you go about getting diagnosed with ADHD? The doctor sees that you have six out of the nine symptoms of inattention or six out of the nine symptoms of hyperactivity. If you're over 17 years old, then you only need five of those nine symptoms. That means you meet the diagnostic criteria and we're going to say you have ADHD. Let's look at the other end of the spectrum. There would be a lengthy interview lasting for an hour to an hour and a half. There would be several questionnaires that are not just looking at a flat rate of how many symptoms you have, but they would be standardized, possibly IQ testing. And then they could do a continuous performance test. Every time a letter flashes on the screen, you got to press a button. And then they might also look at emotional factors. If you have ADHD, there is a higher chance of having either anxiety or depression. Now, the third way to get diagnosed would probably be somewhere in between those
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `15,571 212 14 60`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Diagnosis is based on having 6 out of 9 symptoms of inattention or 6 out of 9 symptoms of hyperactivity(-impulsivity)
  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR's symptom counts and its 6-of-9 threshold for individuals up to age 16.

- **Claim 2:** If you're over 17, you only need 5 of those 9 symptoms
  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR's reduced threshold (5+ symptoms) for adolescents age 17 and older and adults.

- **Claim 3:** Meeting either symptom-count threshold alone means "we're going to say you have ADHD" (implying symptom count is sufficient for diagnosis)
  - **Unsupported.** Symptom count alone is insufficient: onset, persistence, cross-setting presence, impairment, developmental appropriateness, and differential diagnosis are also required.

- **Claim 4:** A more thorough diagnostic approach involves a lengthy interview (roughly 1 to 1.5 hours)
  - **Unsupported.** A comprehensive clinical interview is appropriate, but there is no universal required duration of 60–90 minutes.

- **Claim 5:** Standardized questionnaires (not just raw symptom counts), possible IQ testing, and continuous performance tests (e.g., pressing a button when a letter flashes) may be used
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's mention of standardized rating scales (e.g., Conners', Vanderbilt) as helpful adjuncts, and continuous performance tests (like QbTest) are specifically referenced in the updated NICE guideline as an option for ages 6–17; IQ testing is a reasonable component of a comprehensive differential workup, though not explicitly mandated in the reference materials.

- **Claim 6:** People with ADHD have a higher chance of also having anxiety or depression
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 and NICE both note that ADHD commonly co-occurs with mood disorders and anxiety/fear-related disorders.

- Unsupported claims: **2**
- Supported claims: **4**

  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11/NICE guidance that untreated ADHD is associated with academic, occupational, and interpersonal impairment across the lifespan.

## Video 15 — ADHD Explained in 60 seconds

### Transcript

```text
Ever heard people say ADHD is just about being distracted? Well, that's only scratching the surface. ADHD or attention deficit hyperactivity disorder is a neurodedevelopmental condition that affects focus, impulse control, and emotional regulation. It comes in three types: inattentive, losing track of details, forgetfulness, zoning out, hyperactive impulsive, restlessness, talking a lot, acting before thinking, and combined, a mix of both. It's not about laziness. It's about how the brain manages dopamine, the chemical linked to motivation and reward. Here's something mind-blowing. Rates of ADHD diagnosis have nearly doubled worldwide in the past 20 years, and experts believe it's not because more people suddenly have it, but because awareness and recognition are finally catching up, and treatment, it's no longer just medication. Research is now exploring brain training, digital therapies, lifestyle interventions, and even video games designed to improve attention. One of which is FDA approved for kids. So next time you hear ADHD, remember it's not just about distraction.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `618,665 25,002 1,038 60`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD is "just about being distracted" is an oversimplification — it's more than that
  - **Supported.** Consistent with the broader diagnostic picture, which includes hyperactivity/impulsivity and emotional dysregulation, not just inattention.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD is a neurodevelopmental condition affecting focus, impulse control, and emotional regulation
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 classifies ADHD under neurodevelopmental disorders and lists emotional dysregulation and low frustration tolerance as common associated features alongside the core inattention/hyperactivity-impulsivity symptoms.

- **Claim 3:** ADHD comes in three types — inattentive, hyperactive-impulsive, and combined
  - **Supported.** Matches both DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 presentation specifiers.

- **Claim 4:** Inattentive type involves losing track of details, forgetfulness, zoning out
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention symptom clusters.

- **Claim 5:** Hyperactive-impulsive type involves restlessness, talking a lot, acting before thinking
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 hyperactivity-impulsivity symptom clusters.

- **Claim 6:** "It's not about laziness. It's about how the brain manages dopamine, the chemical linked to motivation and reward"
  - **Unsupported.** Dopamine is implicated in ADHD, but reducing laziness/motivation differences to ‘how the brain manages dopamine’ overstates a complex, heterogeneous evidence base.

- **Claim 7:** Rates of ADHD diagnosis have "nearly doubled worldwide" in the past 20 years
  - **Unsupported.** No specific figure for this is present in the reference materials; ICD-11 gives ADHD childhood prevalence in the range of a few percent, but does not provide a "doubling over 20 years" trend figure, so this specific statistic cannot be verified against the prescribed reference set and would need an epidemiological source to confirm.

- **Claim 8:** The rise is attributed to improved awareness/recognition rather than more people actually having the condition
  - **Unsupported.** Greater recognition is one contributor to diagnosis trends, but the video does not establish that prevalence has not changed or that awareness is the sole explanation.

- **Claim 9:** Treatment is no longer just medication — research is exploring brain training, digital therapies, lifestyle interventions, and video games for attention, one of which is FDA-approved for kids
  - **Supported.** NICE does support non-pharmacological interventions (parent-training, CBT, environmental modifications) alongside medication, and there is a real FDA-authorized video-game-based digital therapeutic for pediatric ADHD attention issues; however, brain training and "digital therapies" broadly are still considered investigational/emerging rather than established evidence-based treatments per NICE's guideline, so framing them as an established part of the ADHD treatment toolkit somewhat overstates their current evidentiary status.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **9**
- Unsupported claims: **3**
- Supported claims: **6**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(9 − 3) / 9 × 100 = 66.7%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

  - **Supported.** Matches DSM-5-TR/ICD-11's functional impairment requirement (symptoms must have a direct negative impact on academic, occupational, or social functioning).

- **Claim 4:** Symptoms aren't better explained by another mental disorder, such as an anxiety disorder or personality disorder
  - **Supported.** Matches the DSM-5-TR/ICD-11 exclusion criterion; ICD-11 specifically names anxiety and fear-related disorders as an example of conditions to differentiate from.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Acting quickly without properly thinking things through
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 impulsivity criterion (acting in response to immediate stimuli without deliberation or consideration of risks/consequences).

- **Claim 2:** Disorganization and problems prioritizing
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criterion (difficulty planning, managing, and organizing tasks and activities).

- **Claim 3:** Poor time management skills
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11 inattention criteria around organizing and completing tasks/activities.

- **Claim 4:** Problems focusing on a task
  - **Supported.** Core ICD-11 inattention symptom (difficulty sustaining attention).

- **Claim 5:** Trouble multitasking
  - **Supported.** Related to executive-function difficulties described in ICD-11, though not named explicitly as "multitasking" in the criteria; a reasonable extension of sustained attention/organization difficulties.

- **Claim 6:** Excessive activity or restlessness
  - **Supported.** Core ICD-11 hyperactivity symptom.

- **Claim 7:** Poor planning
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criterion on organizing/planning tasks.

- **Claim 8:** Getting easily upset or frustrated at seemingly minor challenges
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 lists "low frustration tolerance" as a common associated clinical feature of ADHD.

- **Claim 9:** Frequent mood swings
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 notes "mood lability" as an associated feature of ADHD, though it also cautions that mood symptoms should not be exclusively associated with mood episodes (to differentiate from mood disorders); presented here as a standalone ADHD symptom without that caveat, but the underlying association is real.

- **Claim 10:** Problems following tasks through and completing them
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criterion (not completing tasks).

- **Claim 11:** Having a quick temper
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's noted associated feature of low frustration tolerance/emotional dysregulation.

- **Claim 12:** Trouble coping with stress (cut off in transcript, but discernible)
  - **Supported.** Broadly consistent with ICD-11's description of emotional dysregulation as a common associated feature, though not a core diagnostic criterion itself.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Daydreaming/zoning out, difficulty staying focused on tasks or conversations
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criteria (appearing to daydream, mind elsewhere, difficulty sustaining attention).

- **Claim 2:** Chronic perfectionism, excessive time on details, fear of mistakes
  - **Unsupported.** Perfectionism may be a coping style in some women but is not an established ADHD symptom or women-specific diagnostic marker.

- **Claim 3:** Excessive talking, sometimes without realizing it, leading to interrupting others
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 hyperactivity-impulsivity criterion ("talking too much," "difficulty engaging in activities quietly").

- **Claim 4:** Low self-esteem stemming from comparing oneself to others or feeling inadequate due to ADHD challenges
  - **Supported.** Systematic-review evidence reports lower self-esteem in women with ADHD as an associated outcome, although it is not a diagnostic criterion and the causes vary.

- **Claim 5:** Physical restlessness, needing to move/fidget even when not appropriate
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 hyperactivity criterion (physical restlessness, discomfort with sitting still).

- **Claim 6:** Chronic forgetfulness — forgetting appointments, deadlines, tasks
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criterion (forgetful in daily activities, losing things).

- **Claim 7:** Difficulty maintaining friendships due to impulsive behavior, missed social cues, or emotion-regulation difficulties
  - **Unsupported.** ADHD can strain friendships through impulsivity and emotional dysregulation, but ‘missed social cues’ is not a specific ADHD criterion and the compound statement overgeneralizes.

- **Claim 8:** Emotional impulsivity — reacting quickly/intensely to emotions, leading to outbursts or regret
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's noted associated features of emotional dysregulation and low frustration tolerance in ADHD.

- **Claim 9:** Rejection sensitivity — intense fear of rejection/criticism causing social anxiety or overcompensation
  - **Unsupported.** Sensitivity to criticism may occur, but rejection-sensitive dysphoria is not a DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 diagnosis or validated ADHD symptom.

- **Claim 10:** Chronic overwhelm — feeling overwhelmed by tasks/responsibilities/emotions, leading to burnout or difficulty managing daily demands
  - **Unsupported.** Overwhelm may accompany impairment, but chronic burnout is not an established diagnostic sign and the claim treats a variable experience as characteristic.

- Unsupported claims: **4**
- Supported claims: **6**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Women with ADHD tend to have lower self-esteem
  - **Supported.** Women with ADHD show elevated risk of low self-esteem in systematic-review evidence, but this is an associated outcome rather than a diagnostic symptom.

- **Claim 2:** More likely to be daydreamers
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11's inattentive presentation description, including the note that a "restrictive inattentive pattern" with daydreaming and mind-wandering can be more prominent in a subset, and ICD-11 explicitly notes females are more likely to exhibit inattentive symptoms.

- **Claim 3:** Tend to fidget in their chair a lot
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 hyperactivity criterion (difficulty sitting still, restlessness), though ICD-11 also notes males are more likely than females to show overt hyperactivity-impulsivity symptoms, so this is a somewhat inconsistent pairing with the sex-specific framing of the video.

- **Claim 4:** May deal with odd eating habits or binge eating
  - **Supported.** ADHD is associated with elevated risk of binge-eating and other eating disorders; these remain separate conditions rather than core ADHD symptoms.

- **Claim 5:** More likely to be labeled as "moody" or "too sensitive"
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 does note mood lability and emotional dysregulation as associated features of ADHD, which could lead to such labeling; framed as a social perception rather than a symptom per se, but grounded in a real underlying feature.

- **Claim 6:** Difficulty with time management and organization
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criteria (difficulty planning, managing, organizing tasks and activities).

- **Claim 7:** More sensitive to rejection and criticism
  - **Unsupported.** Rejection sensitivity is not an official ADHD criterion; evidence for an ADHD-specific construct remains limited.

- **Claim 8:** More likely to be anxious and have a "serious case of imposter syndrome"
  - **Unsupported.** Anxiety commonly co-occurs with ADHD, but ‘serious imposter syndrome’ is not an established ADHD feature; the compound claim is therefore unsupported as stated.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** They get obsessed with someone/something and disappear from friends for months at a time (hyperfocus)
  - **Unsupported.** Interest-dependent attention can occur, but obsession with people and disappearing from friends for months is not a validated sign of ADHD in women.

- **Claim 2:** When they achieve something they feel no accomplishment, only relief it's done
  - **Unsupported.** Feeling only relief and no accomplishment is not an established ADHD symptom.

- **Claim 3:** If they have a minor commitment later today, they can't relax and are stuck in "waiting mode" all day
  - **Unsupported.** ‘Waiting mode’ is an anecdotal internet construct, not a diagnostic sign.

- **Claim 4:** They have a strong sense of justice and fairness, reacting strongly when people do mean things
  - **Supported.** The ICD-11-based differential diagnosis reference explicitly links ADHD's emotion regulation problems to "a strong sense of justice and low frustration tolerance".

- **Claim 5:** Their hyperactivity is internalized ("in their heads"), they were never physically hyperactive, and were misdiagnosed with an anxiety disorder
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 does note hyperactivity in adolescents/adults often presents as internal "feelings of physical restlessness" rather than overt motor hyperactivity, giving the internalization idea some basis; however, ICD-11's actual boundary discussion of anxiety and fear-related disorders doesn't support the framing that internalized hyperactivity routinely causes anxiety-disorder misdiagnosis.

- **Claim 6:** Waiting for a slow talker is excruciating because they've already guessed the end of the sentence
  - **Unsupported.** Impatience and interruption are supported, but claiming the person has already guessed another speaker’s sentence is an unsupported cognition claim.

- **Claim 7:** They're highly driven and overachieving, but chronically overwhelmed and near burnout
  - **Unsupported.** Some women compensate or mask, but being highly driven, overachieving, and chronically near burnout is not a general ADHD sign.

- **Claim 8:** They'll sit on their best friend's couch once a month to give moral support so the friend can clean
  - **Unsupported.** Body doubling may be used as a practical strategy, but this exact monthly couch scenario is not a symptom by which ADHD in women can be identified.

- **Claim 9:** They excitedly call people, then ignore callbacks because "the dopamine has left their body"
  - **Unsupported.** Changing interest can affect follow-through, but saying ‘the dopamine has left the body’ is biologically inaccurate and reductive.

- **Claim 10:** They'll try to Google something, forget what it was, and check recent apps to find the trigger
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criteria (being easily distracted, forgetful in daily activities, losing track of tasks).

- **Claim 11:** After a day of masking, they overthink every social interaction and engage in harsh self-talk ("that person must hate me now")
  - **Unsupported.** Masking and post-interaction rumination may occur, but the asserted sequence and ‘they must hate me’ response are not established ADHD criteria.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **11**
- Unsupported claims: **8**
- Supported claims: **3**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(11 − 8) / 11 × 100 = 27.3%**
- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

## Video 26 — These ADHD-Like Struggles Aren’t Always ADHD #shorts

### Transcript

```text
These ADHD-like struggles are always ADHD. You lose the threat in meeting. You can't start until the deadline is breathing down your neck and switching task feels much harder than it should. You start wondering, do I have ADHD? Maybe, but stress, short sleep, low mood, pain, medication effects, or just simply carrying too much can also make your executive skills less reliable. ADHD is a developmental condition, so the pattern usually starts in childhood and affects more than one part of your life. But sometimes adult responsibilities expose a pattern that you've been compensating for all along. So, look at the timeline. Has this followed you since childhood? Does it show up at work, at home, and in daily life? Or did it begin when your workload, your health, or your sleep changed? One overload season doesn't diagnose ADHD, but a lifelong pattern may deserve a closer evaluation.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `15,826 1,120 28 54`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Losing the thread in meetings, being unable to start tasks until deadline pressure, and difficulty switching tasks can occur without ADHD being the cause
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's "boundary with normality" and differential diagnosis sections, which note that inattention/executive symptoms are common in many people and must be distinguished from other conditions before ADHD is diagnosed.

- **Claim 2:** Stress, short sleep, low mood, pain, medication effects, or being overloaded can make executive skills less reliable (mimicking ADHD)
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 explicitly lists boundaries with mood disorders, anxiety/fear-related disorders, medication effects (e.g., certain prescribed medications), and other medical conditions (e.g., sleep-wake disorders) as causes of attention/executive symptoms that can resemble but are distinct from ADHD.

- **Claim 3:** ADHD is a developmental condition, so the pattern usually starts in childhood and affects more than one part of life
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 essential features require evidence of symptoms prior to age 12 and require symptoms to be evident across multiple situations/settings (e.g., home, school, work).

- **Claim 4:** Adult responsibilities can expose a lifelong pattern the person had been compensating for
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 explicitly notes some individuals may first present in adulthood, and demands exceeding capacity to compensate is a recognized presentation; NICE also notes symptoms may only become limiting with increasing demands.

- **Claim 5:** To evaluate whether it's ADHD, look at the timeline — whether it followed you since childhood and shows up at work, home, and daily life, versus only starting when workload/health/sleep changed
  - **Supported.** Directly reflects ICD-11's requirement of childhood onset and cross-situational pervasiveness as key diagnostic distinguishers.

- **Claim 6:** One overload season doesn't diagnose ADHD, but a lifelong pattern may deserve closer evaluation
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's boundary-with-normality section and NICE's guidance that a full clinical assessment (not a single presentation) is needed for diagnosis.

## Video 27 — How to Diagnose ADHD

### Transcript

```text
[music] Finally, a scan that can detect ADHD. No, that's not a thing. Let's talk about why. Hi, I'm Dr. Mona. I make sense of all things child development, parenting, and health. So, if you're not already following, click that follow button. Now, I get it. When you're concerned about your child's development, or behavior, the idea of one quick test that gives you answers sounds amazing. But ADHD isn't something a machine can pick up. If there were, mainstream medicine would have incorporated it into practice. And especially as a DO or osteopathic physician, I'm always curious about the mind body connection. But ADHD is a neurodedevelopmental condition, not stress stuck in the nervous system. The brain is wired a little differently, especially in the areas that control attention, activity level, and impulse control. And here's something every parent should know. Most experts don't diagnose ADHD before age four. And that child in the video definitely looks under four. Why? Because toddlers are still developing. It's neurotypical at 2 or 3 years old to be distractable, impulsive, and full of energy. That's development, not ADHD. So, when you see someone offering a neurological insight scan to find the real cause of ADHD behaviors, here's what that really is. These scans measure things like muscle tension, skin temperature, and heart rate patterns. None of that can tell you anything about ADHD, attention, or brain wiring. They look fancy and medical, but they don't diagnose anything. And if your active child is running around, which many are, it can pick up stress that is typical for an active kid. Think of them as a stress snapshot, not a map of your child's wiring or their brain. And worse, they're often marketed to worried parents. Parents who just want answers when no legitimate medical organization recognizes these scans as diagnostic for anything. Here's what we actually know about ADHD. ADHD often runs in families. It shows up in more than one setting, at home, school, or social situations. Diagnosis is based on history and patterns and not machines or marketing. So, when you see a colorful scan claiming to find the root cause of ADHD, take it as a red flag. What your child needs isn't a gimmick. It's understanding, patience, and an evaluation by people who actually study child development. ADHD isn't about broken wiring. It's about different wiring. And when kids get the right support, they can thrive.
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `9,124 380 11 147`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** No scan/machine can detect or diagnose ADHD
  - **Supported.** NICE 1.3.1/1.3.2 establish diagnosis is based on full clinical/psychosocial assessment, developmental and psychiatric history, and observer reports; rating scales are only "helpful added tools" and diagnosis should not be made solely on rating scale or observational data, let alone a scan.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD is a neurodevelopmental condition involving different brain wiring in areas controlling attention, activity level, and impulse control (not "stress stuck in the nervous system")
  - **Supported.** ADHD is classified under "Neurodevelopmental disorders" in ICD-11, and its essential features center on inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity as core, brain-based domains.

- **Claim 3:** Most experts don't diagnose ADHD before age 4
  - **Supported.** AAP guidance provides a diagnostic/treatment framework beginning at age 4; behavior at ages 2–3 is especially difficult to distinguish from normal development, so the claim is reasonable rather than an absolute prohibition.

- **Claim 4:** It's neurotypical for 2-3 year olds to be distractible, impulsive, and full of energy — that's development, not ADHD
  - **Supported.** Aligns with ICD-11's "boundary with normality (threshold)" section, which notes inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity symptoms are present in many children especially during certain developmental periods such as early childhood, and only qualify as ADHD when persistent, pervasive, and significantly out of keeping with developmental level.

- **Claim 5:** The described "neurological insight" scans (measuring muscle tension, skin temperature, heart rate) cannot tell you anything about ADHD, attention, or brain wiring
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's diagnostic process, which contains no such physiological/stress-based measures as diagnostic criteria or tools for ADHD.

- **Claim 6:** No legitimate medical organization recognizes these scans as diagnostic for ADHD
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's requirement that ADHD be diagnosed only via clinical assessment, history, and observer reports by trained specialists, with no scan-based diagnostic pathway referenced anywhere in NICE or ICD-11.

- **Claim 7:** ADHD often runs in families
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 states ADHD tends to run in families, with evidence of high heritability.

- **Claim 8:** ADHD shows up in more than one setting — home, school, or social situations
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 requires manifestations of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity to be evident across multiple situations or settings.

- **Claim 9:** Diagnosis is based on history and patterns, not machines or marketing
  - **Supported.** Directly matches NICE 1.3.1's diagnostic basis (full clinical/psychosocial assessment, developmental/psychiatric history, observer reports).

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **9**
- Unsupported claims: **0**
- Supported claims: **9**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Stimulant medications like Adderall carry high concerns around diversion (being sold/misused rather than taken as prescribed)
  - **Supported.** NICE explicitly addresses this risk (1.7.23–1.7.24, 1.8.19), instructing caution when prescribing stimulants if there's risk of diversion and ongoing monitoring for stimulant misuse/diversion.

- **Claim 2:** Doctors/parents always have to be vigilant about not over-medicating children with ADHD
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's cautious, staged approach to prescribing in children (1.5.13), which only offers medication after environmental modifications have been tried and a full baseline assessment completed.

- **Claim 3:** The DSM criteria for ADHD have changed a lot over the past several decades
  - **Supported.** NICE's context section explicitly contrasts the "previous, less stringent DSM-IV criteria" with the newer DSM-5 criteria, confirming meaningful revision over time.

- **Claim 4:** The criteria have gotten "softer," making ADHD easier to diagnose now
  - **Supported.** NICE's context section notes childhood prevalence rates under DSM-IV were 3–9% and "these may increase under the new DSM-5 criteria," which supports a broadening trend, though "softer" is a loose, informal overgeneralization of a more nuanced criteria change (e.g., age-of-onset threshold raised from 7 to 12).

## Video 30 — Diagnostic Criteria ADHD #adhd

### Transcript

```text
this is the diagnostic criteria for ADHD in the UK so there are two potential presentations of ADHD in attentive type and hyperactive and impulsive type and within each section there are a list of diagnostic criteria in the inattentive type it's things like being unable to complete tasks struggling to follow verbal instructions losing things in the hyperactive and impulsive type it's things like talking a lot struggling to wait your turn in a queue being unable to sit still even in a quiet place where you would be expected to sit still now if you are to be diagnosed with one of those presentations you need to tick six of those different traits within that section to get that presentation diagnosis if you tick six within both of those sections you would be diagnosed like me with combined type which means that you have both presentations and in addition if you are an adult it needs to be considered to have a moderate impact on your life that is that you might struggle with things like keeping a job maintaining relationships
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `26,800 1,581 39 58`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** There are two potential presentations of ADHD — inattentive type and hyperactive/impulsive type
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 lists exactly these specifiers: predominantly inattentive presentation (6A05.0) and predominantly hyperactive-impulsive presentation (6A05.1).

- **Claim 2:** Inattentive-type criteria include things like being unable to complete tasks, struggling to follow verbal instructions, and losing things
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention clusters: "not completing tasks," "often seeming not to listen when spoken to directly," and "losing things".

- **Claim 3:** Hyperactive/impulsive-type criteria include talking a lot, struggling to wait your turn in a queue, and being unable to sit still even where expected to
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 hyperactivity-impulsivity clusters: "talking too much," "difficulty waiting their turn... in conversation, games or activities," and "difficulty sitting still without fidgeting"/discomfort with sitting still.

- **Claim 4:** You need to "tick six" traits within a section to receive that presentation's diagnosis
  - **Unsupported.** Adults require five, not six, symptoms under DSM-5-TR; ICD-11 does not use this fixed six-item rule.

- **Claim 5:** Meeting six criteria in both sections results in a "combined type" diagnosis, meaning both presentations are present
  - **Unsupported.** Combined presentation follows the applicable threshold in both clusters; it is not always six in each, especially for adults.

- **Claim 6:** If you are an adult, it needs to be considered to have at least a moderate impact on your life (e.g., struggling to keep a job or maintain relationships)
  - **Supported.** NICE 1.3.3 does require symptoms to "cause at least moderate psychological, social, or educational or occupational impairment," which matches the substance of the claim; however, NICE applies this moderate-impairment threshold to ADHD diagnosis generally, not as an adult-specific add-on requirement as the video implies.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **2**
- Supported claims: **4**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(6 − 2) / 6 × 100 = 66.7%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADD/ADHD "is not one thing" like depression or anxiety, implying distinct brain "types" that his own ADD type test can identify
  - **Unsupported.** The speaker’s proprietary brain ‘types’ are not recognized ADHD presentations and are not established by the attached diagnostic references.

- **Claim 2:** Stimulants shouldn't be given to everyone with ADD — "for the right brain they're miraculous, for the wrong brain they're a nightmare," which is why stimulants have a bad reputation
  - **Unsupported.** Medication response is individualized, but the ‘right brain/wrong brain’ classification and ‘nightmare’ rule are not validated clinical criteria.

- **Claim 3:** Stop "medicating" ADHD with alcohol and marijuana because they "steal your dopamine"
  - **Unsupported.** ADHD is associated with substance misuse, but alcohol and cannabis do not simply ‘steal dopamine’; the compound mechanism is unsupported.

- **Claim 4:** Limit gadgets and video games because they "steal your dopamine"
  - **Unsupported.** Problematic digital-media use is associated with ADHD, but devices do not literally ‘steal dopamine.’.

- **Claim 5:** Exercise should be recommended for ADHD (boosts dopamine)
  - **Supported.** NICE 1.6.1 explicitly recommends stressing the value of regular exercise for people with ADHD (the "boosts dopamine" mechanism itself isn't stated in NICE, but the recommendation to exercise is supported).

- **Claim 6:** A higher-protein, lower-simple-carbohydrate diet is recommended
  - **Unsupported.** NICE recommends a balanced diet, not a general high-protein/low-simple-carbohydrate ADHD prescription.

- **Claim 7:** L-tyrosine ("elyroine") helps because it boosts dopamine
  - **Unsupported.** L-tyrosine is not an established guideline-supported ADHD treatment.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Often fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes in schoolwork, work, or other activities
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention cluster: "lacking attention to detail; making careless mistakes in school or work assignments".

- **Claim 2:** Often has trouble holding attention on tasks or play activities
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "having difficulty sustaining attention on tasks that do not provide a high level of stimulation or reward".

- **Claim 3:** Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly
  - **Supported.** Directly matches ICD-11 wording: "often seeming not to listen when spoken to directly".

- **Claim 4:** Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or workplace duties
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "not completing tasks" and general inattention criteria around task follow-through.

- **Claim 5:** Often has trouble organizing tasks and activities
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "having difficulty planning, managing and organizing schoolwork, tasks and other activities".

- **Claim 6:** Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks requiring sustained mental effort
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "difficulty sustaining attention on tasks that... require sustained mental effort".

- **Claim 7:** Often loses things necessary for tasks/activities (keys, wallet, phone, pencils, tools, books)
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "losing things".

- **Claim 8:** Easily distractible and often forgetful in daily activities
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 almost verbatim: "being easily distracted by extraneous stimuli," "being forgetful in daily activities".

- **Claim 9:** There are also hyperactive symptoms, including inability to sit still and being fidgety
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 hyperactivity-impulsivity cluster: "having difficulty sitting still without fidgeting".

- **Claim 10:** Part of the diagnostic criteria is that some symptoms must be present prior to age 12
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 explicitly states "evidence of significant inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity symptoms prior to age 12"; NICE's context section also confirms DSM-5 uses age 12 as the threshold.

  - **Supported.** ICD-11's "boundary with normality (threshold)" explicitly states that inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity symptoms are present in many people, especially during certain developmental periods, and that a diagnosis requires these symptoms to be persistent across time, pervasive across situations, and significantly out of keeping with developmental level with direct negative functional impact — casual self-application of the label does not meet this threshold.

- **Claim 2:** There is overdiagnosis of ADHD happening in the population
  - **Supported.** A 2021 systematic scoping review of 334 studies found evidence of ADHD overdiagnosis and overtreatment in children and adolescents, while noting important evidence gaps; the statement is supportable with that population qualification.

  - **Unsupported.** ICD-11 does not specify a fixed count of "14 required behaviours" for diagnosis, nor does it (or DSM-5-TR, as referenced in NICE's context section) require meeting all listed symptoms; diagnosis requires "several symptoms" from inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity clusters, not a complete checklist of every possible item — this framing materially misrepresents how the criteria work.

  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "difficulty sitting still without fidgeting".

- **Claim 3:** Quickly distracted by different stimulating things
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "being easily distracted by extraneous stimuli or thoughts not related to the task at hand".

- **Claim 4:** Difficulty concentrating on things that don't interest you
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "difficulty sustaining attention on tasks that do not provide a high level of stimulation or reward".

- **Claim 5:** Quickly switching from one activity to another
  - **Unsupported.** Rapidly switching activities is not itself one of the official DSM-5-TR/ICD-11 checklist symptoms; it may arise in some people but cannot be presented as a required behavior.

  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11's note on "feelings of physical restlessness" in adolescents/adults.

  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "having difficulty waiting their turn in conversation, games or activities".

- **Claim 8:** Often knowing/blurting out the answer to a question
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "blurting out answers in school".

- **Claim 9:** Trouble fulfilling/completing tasks
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "not completing tasks".

- **Claim 10:** Difficulty trying to play calmly
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "having difficulty engaging in activities quietly".

- **Claim 11:** Often not thinking through before doing things
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "acting in response to immediate stimuli without deliberation... impulsive decisions".

- **Claim 12:** Often losing things
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 directly: "losing things".

- **Claim 13:** Frequently disrupting/interrupting others
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "interrupting or intruding on others' conversations or games".

- **Claim 14:** Talking a lot
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "talking too much".

- **Claim 15:** Often not seeming to be listening
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 directly: "often seeming not to listen when spoken to directly".

## Video 36 — Things that are common after getting a late ADHD Diagnosis: 1

It is super normal to experience grief, anger, feeling overwhelm about where do I even start dealing with this? And even noticing that your ADHD symptoms are worsening. Totally, totally normal. And I promise you, it's temporary. But here's what I recommend you consider doing after that late diagnosis. First off, explore treatment options. Talk to your doctor about medication and lifestyle changes that can aid in ADHD management. And then from there, I recommend taking time to learn about your ADHD. This is so important because when we understand our ADHD brains, it helps us to manage our ADHD better, advocate for ourselves more effectively, be kinder to ourselves, and it gives us the agency we need to make the changes in our lives that need to happen for us to be more successful. There's a ton of information about ADHD on the internet. Not all of it's true. So, I definitely recommend check your sources. And here are a few reliable sources that I really like. And the third thing that I recommend you do is that you find some sort of community for support. A lot of times when we get that ADHD diagnosis, we can feel really alone. And it was so validating to be able to connect with people who also had ADHD. Now, if you don't have ADHD friends, there's tons of places for online communities. Add.org or has some really good support groups. ### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** 6% of US adults have ADHD
  - **Supported.** CDC data estimate that 6.0% of U.S. adults had a current ADHD diagnosis in 2023.

- **Claim 2:** Over half of adults with ADHD are diagnosed in adulthood
  - **Supported.** The same CDC analysis reports that 55.9% of adults with current ADHD were first diagnosed at age 18 or older.

- **Claim 3:** It's "totally normal" to experience grief, anger, and feeling overwhelmed after a late diagnosis, and to notice ADHD symptoms seeming to worsen, and that this is temporary
  - **Unsupported.** Grief or anger after a late diagnosis may occur, but it is not established that symptoms normally worsen temporarily or that this trajectory is universal.

- **Claim 4:** Recommends exploring treatment, including talking to a doctor about medication and lifestyle changes for ADHD management
  - **Supported.** Matches NICE's general treatment framework, which includes medication (1.5.15) and lifestyle factors such as diet and exercise (1.6.1) as part of comprehensive ADHD management.

- **Claim 5:** Learning about/understanding your ADHD helps you manage it better and advocate for yourself more effectively
  - **Supported.** Matches NICE 1.4.3, which recommends discussing with newly diagnosed people the positive impacts of diagnosis, including "improving their understanding of symptoms" and "identifying and building on individual strengths".

- **Claim 6:** Finding community/support groups is recommended after a late diagnosis
  - **Supported.** Matches NICE 1.4.4, which recommends informing people receiving an ADHD diagnosis about sources of information including "local and national support groups and voluntary organisations".

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **1**
- Supported claims: **5**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(6 − 1) / 6 × 100 = 83.3%**
- **Corrected label: Label 1 — Accurate**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD is essentially a "superpower" — defined as being able to concentrate only on things you enjoy, and not on things you don't enjoy
  - **Unsupported.** This materially misrepresents ADHD. While ICD-11 does note that "inattention may not be evident when the individual is engaged in activities that provide intense stimulation and frequent rewards," this is one feature among many, not a definition of the disorder. Framing ADHD as an enjoyable-vs-boring dichotomy and a "superpower" omits the required cross-setting functional impairment, and overgeneralizes a partial truth into a false overall characterization.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD needs to be negatively impacting your quality of life for you to even receive a diagnosis
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11's essential features (symptoms must have "a direct negative impact on academic, occupational or social functioning") and NICE 1.3.3, which requires "at least moderate psychological, social, or educational or occupational impairment" for diagnosis.

- **Claim 3:** A brain-imaging response-inhibition task shows a non-ADHD brain with substantial activation in relevant regions
  - **Supported.** Group-level fMRI research has found differences in activation during inhibitory-control tasks between ADHD and control groups; this does not diagnose an individual.

- **Claim 4:** Giving a non-ADHD brain methylphenidate produces some activation change on this same task
  - **Unsupported.** The exact depicted claim about methylphenidate changing activation in a non-ADHD brain is not sourced and cannot be verified from the general meta-analytic evidence.

- **Claim 5:** The same task performed by an ADHD brain shows markedly less activation in the relevant region
  - **Supported.** Meta-analytic neuroimaging research supports group-level underactivation in inhibition/attention networks in ADHD, with substantial heterogeneity.

- **Claim 6:** Giving stimulant medication to the ADHD brain produces a "significant increase" in activation
  - **Supported.** Stimulant treatment can partially normalize task-related activation in some group studies; this is a group-level tendency, not a universal scan result.

- **Claim 7:** People with ADHD have different dopamine regulation and structurally different brains
  - **Supported.** ADHD research supports altered catecholamine signaling and group-level structural differences, but neither finding is an individual diagnostic biomarker.

- **Claim 8:** ADHD is heritable
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 explicitly states ADHD "tends to run in families, with evidence of high heritability".

- **Claim 9:** There are decades of peer-reviewed, high-quality research supporting ADHD's existence as a legitimate diagnosis
  - **Supported.** Consistent with the existence of well-established, evidence-reviewed diagnostic systems (ICD-11) and evidence-based clinical guidelines (NICE) built explicitly on systematic evidence reviews.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **9**
- Unsupported claims: **2**
- Supported claims: **7**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(9 − 2) / 9 × 100 = 77.8%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**
