### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** There's no clinical test for ADHD — no blood test, no CT scan
  - **Supported.** NICE's diagnostic process (1.3.1) relies on full clinical/psychosocial assessment, developmental and psychiatric history, and observer reports; 1.3.2 explicitly states diagnosis should not be made solely on rating scale or observational data, and no blood test, CT scan, or other biomarker test is listed anywhere in NICE or ICD-11 as part of the diagnostic process.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD diagnosis is "based on someone's opinion about your child," implying it's merely subjective rather than a legitimate clinical determination
  - **Unsupported.** This overstates and misrepresents the diagnostic process. NICE 1.3.1 requires diagnosis to be made only by "a specialist psychiatrist, paediatrician or other appropriately qualified healthcare professional with training and expertise," based on a full clinical/psychosocial assessment, structured developmental/psychiatric history, and observer reports across settings — a structured clinical evaluation process, not mere personal opinion.

- **Claim 3:** Using medication for a behavior problem doesn't solve the problem, it just masks a symptom
  - **Unsupported.** This contradicts the evidence-based framing in NICE, which presents medication as a clinically effective treatment for ADHD symptoms and impairment (1.5.13, 1.5.15, 1.7.27 — dose titration is guided toward "reduced symptoms, positive behaviour change, improvements in education, employment and relationships"), not as symptom-masking; ADHD is also classified as a neurodevelopmental disorder (ICD-11) rather than a "behavior problem," so this framing misrepresents both the condition and the treatment's evidenced purpose.

- **Claim 4:** There are always side effects (implying medication universally causes adverse effects)
  - **Unsupported.** NICE does extensively document potential adverse effects of ADHD medications (weight/height changes, cardiovascular effects, tics, sleep changes, sexual dysfunction with atomoxetine, etc. — sections 1.8.1–1.8.19), so acknowledging side effects exist has a basis; however, "always" overstates it, since NICE frames these as effects to monitor and manage, not universal or inevitable outcomes for every individual.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **4**
- Unsupported claims: **3**
- Supported claims: **1**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(4 − 3) / 4 × 100 = 25.0%**
- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

NICE 1.3.1 requires diagnosis to be made only by a trained specialist based on full clinical assessment, developmental history, and cross-setting observation — explicitly not something addressable by parental discipline alone, and NICE's "boundary with normality" criteria (via ICD-11) already account for and exclude behavior that is simply typical variation, undermining the claim that legitimate diagnoses are just mislabeled discipline failures.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** What makes ADHD a disorder is that it is causing "disorder" — i.e., it is impairing the person, not merely the presence of symptoms
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 essential features, which require symptoms to have "a direct negative impact on academic, occupational or social functioning," and the "boundary with normality" section, which states symptoms must be "significantly out of keeping with developmental level, and have a direct negative impact" to qualify as a disorder.

- **Claim 2:** There are 18 symptoms on the official checklist
  - **Supported.** The attached ICD-11 reference does not present symptoms as a fixed numbered checklist (it groups them into qualitative clusters under inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity), so the specific number "18" is not verifiable against the ICD-11 material provided; this figure corresponds to the DSM-5 symptom count (9 inattentive + 9 hyperactive-impulsive) rather than anything stated in the attached ICD-11-based documents, so it is not confirmable from the prescribed reference set as given.

- **Claim 3:** Symptoms should have been present even in childhood
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 essential features: "evidence of significant inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity symptoms prior to age 12".

- **Claim 4:** These symptoms (forgetfulness, losing things, difficulty keeping track of things, interrupting, etc.) happen to everybody sometimes, but what distinguishes ADHD is that they are constant/persistent rather than occasional
  - **Supported.** Directly matches ICD-11's "boundary with normality (threshold)" section: symptoms "are present in many children, adolescents and adults, especially during certain developmental periods" but diagnosis requires them to "be persistent across time, pervasive across situations and significantly out of keeping with developmental level".

- **Claim 5:** A person might not perceive their own symptoms as impairing because they're "too close to it," and checking in with loved ones can reveal whether it's actually a problem
  - **Supported.** Aligns with ICD-11's additional clinical features, which note "the report of a significant other, family member or co-worker can provide important additional information" in adults, and NICE 1.3.1's requirement for observer reports as part of diagnosis.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD doesn't invent new problems, it exacerbates universal ones — everyone gets distracted, forgets things, and procrastinates sometimes, and this isn't unique to people with ADHD
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's "boundary with normality (threshold)" section, which explicitly states inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity symptoms "are present in many children, adolescents and adults, especially during certain developmental periods," confirming these experiences are universal rather than unique to ADHD.

- **Claim 2:** The key difference is that people with ADHD have these struggles more often/more frequently
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11's requirement that symptoms be "persistent" over time (e.g., over at least 6 months) to distinguish ADHD from typical occasional experiences.

- **Claim 3:** The struggles may have a bigger impact on the person's life
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11's essential features requiring symptoms to have "a direct negative impact on academic, occupational or social functioning," and NICE's requirement of "at least moderate... impairment" (1.3.3).

- **Claim 4:** The struggles might be more visible to others
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's requirement that manifestations be "evident across multiple situations or settings," implying observable impact across contexts rather than being purely internal or unnoticed.

- **Claim 5:** People with ADHD might have stronger emotional feelings about these struggles
  - **Supported.** Consistent with the ICD-11-based differential-diagnosis reference material noting "emotional dysregulation, low frustration tolerance" as common associated features of ADHD.

- **Claim 6:** The overall difference between people with and without ADHD is one of degree, not of kind
  - **Supported.** This framing is well-supported by the cumulative weight of the ICD-11 "boundary with normality" section, which frames the disorder as a matter of symptoms being significantly more persistent, pervasive, and impairing than typical variation — i.e., a difference in degree/severity rather than a categorically distinct experience.

  - **Supported.** ADHD is classified in ICD-11 as a neurodevelopmental disorder involving genuine impairment, not a motivational deficit; this directly counters the "laziness" myth, consistent with the disorder's classification and essential features describing symptoms as outside the person's typical control rather than effort-based.

  - **Supported.** Overcommitting can follow planning, time-management, and impulsivity difficulties in adults with ADHD, but it is a possible manifestation rather than a diagnostic criterion.

  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criteria: "having difficulty planning, managing and organizing schoolwork, tasks and other activities," which encompasses time-management difficulty.

  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "being forgetful in daily activities" and "having difficulty remembering to complete upcoming daily tasks or activities".

  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's additional clinical features, which state ADHD "can also strain interpersonal relationships across the lifespan, including those with family members, peers and romantic partners".

- **Claim 1:** ADHD is not a "superpower" — for many people it is a serious, debilitating struggle rather than a gift
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's classification of ADHD as a disorder requiring significant functional impairment for diagnosis; the essential features require symptoms to have "a direct negative impact on academic, occupational or social functioning," which supports characterizing it as a genuine struggle rather than simply a positive trait.

- **Claim 2:** People with ADHD might start many projects but not finish them, because starting is exciting but follow-through is hard
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criteria: "not completing tasks" and "difficulty sustaining attention on tasks that... require sustained mental effort".

- **Claim 3:** Struggling to focus when it matters most, leading to missed deadlines and forgotten appointments
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11: "having difficulty remembering to complete upcoming daily tasks or activities" and general inattention/sustained-focus criteria.

- **Claim 4:** ADHD can make it harder to keep jobs, maintain relationships, or do basic daily tasks
  - **Supported.** Directly matches ICD-11's additional clinical features: "Adults with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder often find it difficult to hold down a demanding job... ADHD can also strain interpersonal relationships across the lifespan".

- **Claim 5:** Impulsivity can lead to reckless spending, risky decisions, and higher rates of car accidents
  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11's impulsivity cluster, which explicitly lists "impulsive decisions; reckless driving" as an example, and the additional features note ADHD is "associated with an increased risk of physical health problems including accidents".

- **Claim 6:** There is significant emotional toll — overwhelm, burnout, and a persistent feeling of falling behind
  - **Unsupported.** ADHD can cause substantial emotional burden, but ‘burnout’ is not an established ADHD symptom and the compound statement overgeneralizes.

- **Claim 7:** Calling ADHD a "superpower" creates a false expectation and can make people with ADHD blame themselves for struggling, as if they're just "not using their powers correctly"
  - **Unsupported.** The possible psychological effect of ‘superpower’ rhetoric is plausible but not established as a general causal outcome in the cited evidence.

```text
I want y'all to really think about this for a second. So, the problem with ADHD is it is a sensory attentional disorder. So, for a neurotypical person, the more important something is, the more it sinks in. But for ADHD, if we don't ever hear it in the first place, it doesn't matter how important it is because we never heard it. So, people who are neurotypical will also forget things. They won't pay attention 100% of the time, but they don't forget exams. They don't forget projects. They don't forget weddings, right? they don't forget like the big stuff. So, some of that stuff is intact in neurotypical people. So, everyone will forget, but it doesn't happen as often and the consequences are not as severe. When we are relaxed, we make mistakes. So let's constantly be in a state of dread.
```

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD is fundamentally a "sensory attentional disorder" where, unlike neurotypical people, information doesn't "sink in" more just because it's important — if it isn't perceived in the first place, importance is irrelevant
  - **Unsupported.** This specific mechanistic model of ADHD (a sensory-encoding failure rather than importance-based prioritization) is not described in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; ADHD is classified as a neurodevelopmental disorder involving inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity clusters, not framed in the attached references as a distinct "sensory attentional" processing deficit.

- **Claim 2:** Neurotypical people also forget things and don't pay attention 100% of the time, but they don't forget major events like exams, projects, or weddings
  - **Unsupported.** People without ADHD can also forget major events; the categorical contrast is false even though ADHD can increase frequency and impairment.

- **Claim 3:** People with ADHD forget things (including significant things) more often than neurotypical people, and the consequences tend to be more severe
  - **Supported.** The frequency component aligns with ICD-11's "boundary with normality" framing (symptoms like forgetfulness occur in everyone but must be persistent/frequent to indicate ADHD) and its inattention criteria ("being forgetful in daily activities"); however, the specific claim about consequence severity being systematically worse is not something ICD-11 or NICE quantifies or states directly.

- **Claim 4:** Because consequences become severe and frequent, people with ADHD develop a constant "dread mode" or "paranoia mode," being unable to trust themselves when relaxed, making mistakes when relaxed, and living in a persistent state of dread
  - **Unsupported.** This specific psychological/emotional mechanism is not described in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; while emotional dysregulation and low frustration tolerance are noted as associated features, a chronic "dread state" tied to self-distrust when relaxed is not part of the diagnostic or clinical picture in the attached references.

## Video 49 — ADHD and Time Blindness

### Transcript

```text
let's look at a fascinating study the light bulb experiment so Dr Russell Barkley a leading expert on ADHD conducted an experiment to explore time perception in individuals with and without ADHD he put participants in a room and showed them a light bulb and he would turn it on for a time and then off again and he'd asked them to estimate how long it had been on and he found something interesting both the ADHD and the non-adhd participants were able to estimate the amount of time the light was on in about the same range of accuracy but here's where things fell apart for the participants with ADHD when Dr Barkley gave them the light switch and asked them to reproduce the time intervals themselves the adhders were not able to reliably reproduce the same time interval with the light as the group with no ADHD symptoms and so adhders were less capable of using their minds to govern their actions around time- sensitive tasks they could perceive time to a varying degree but they could not judge or effectively estimate the amount of time an event or task would take for them to complete
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `100,262 6,078 154 61`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Dr. Russell Barkley conducted a "light bulb experiment" studying time perception in people with and without ADHD, turning a light on/off and asking participants to estimate how long it had been on
  - **Supported.** Confirmed by multiple independent sources describing this exact methodology, including a clinical trial protocol referencing the same light-bulb-based time reproduction paradigm (Hurks et al., 2011) and multiple secondary sources describing Barkley's original light bulb study design.

- **Claim 2:** Both ADHD and non-ADHD participants were able to estimate the amount of time the light was on with roughly similar accuracy (i.e., no significant difference in time estimation)
  - **Supported.** Directly confirmed by a peer-reviewed Springer study co-authored by Barkley: "manifested more impaired time reproduction, but not time estimation, than did the CC [control] group," and further corroborated by a 2001 literature summary noting "most have found no deficits in time estimation".

- **Claim 3:** When given the light switch and asked to reproduce the same time interval themselves, ADHD participants could not reliably reproduce it as accurately as non-ADHD participants (i.e., a deficit in time reproduction, not perception)
  - **Supported.** Directly confirmed by the same peer-reviewed study: ADHD participants showed "more impaired time reproduction... than did the CC group," and this finding is described as having been replicated (with some failures to replicate noted) across multiple studies per the 2001 Barkley summary.

- **Claim 4:** This shows that people with ADHD are less capable of using their minds to govern their actions around time-sensitive tasks — they can perceive time to a degree, but struggle to judge/estimate how much time a task will take them to complete
  - **Unsupported.** Interval-reproduction findings do not directly prove that people with ADHD cannot estimate future task duration or broadly ‘use their minds to govern actions’; this overextends a limited laboratory result.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **4**
- Unsupported claims: **1**
- Supported claims: **3**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(4 − 1) / 4 × 100 = 75.0%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

## Video 52 — ADHD and Time Blindness Explained

### Transcript

```text
Time blindness is one of the most frustrating ADHD symptoms. And here's why it happens. ADHD brains have trouble sensing the passing of time. It's about how the ADHD brain processes time, motivation, and dopamine. And the fix isn't shame, it's structure. Using timers, visual clocks, or even accountability partners helps bring time back into view, so you're not relying on your brain alone. So, if you've ever wondered why time just disappears with ADHD, now you know. If you found value in this video, follow me for more ADHD tips like
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `8,137 470 19 50`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Time blindness is one of the most frustrating ADHD symptoms
  - **Supported.** While "time blindness" isn't formal DSM-5/ICD-11 terminology, it's widely recognized by ADHD researchers/clinicians as a core feature per external sources, and closely maps onto ICD-11's inattention criteria (difficulty planning, managing, organizing tasks/activities).

- **Claim 2:** ADHD brains have trouble sensing the passing of time
  - **Supported.** Confirmed by Barkley's research (referenced across multiple independent sources) showing impaired time perception/reproduction in ADHD.

- **Claim 3:** Instead of a continuous flow, time feels like two categories — "now" or "not now"
  - **Unsupported.** ‘Now/not now’ is a popular explanatory metaphor, not a validated description of how every ADHD brain represents time.

- **Claim 4:** This is why someone can hyperfocus for hours on something enjoyable but completely lose track of an appointment they were supposed to leave for 30 minutes ago
  - **Supported.** Consistent with both the externally verified "now vs not now" research and ICD-11's own note that "inattention may not be evident when the individual is engaged in activities that provide intense stimulation and frequent rewards"; losing track of appointments also matches ICD-11's inattention criteria around forgetfulness and difficulty managing tasks.

- **Claim 5:** It's not about being careless — it's about how the ADHD brain processes time, motivation, and dopamine
  - **Unsupported.** ADHD-related time-management difficulty is real, but the video’s unified time–motivation–dopamine mechanism is reductive and not established as stated.

- **Claim 6:** The fix isn't shame, it's structure — using timers, visual clocks, or accountability partners helps bring time back into view
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's broader emphasis on "environmental modifications" (NICE terminology) to reduce ADHD impact, and directly corroborated by external sources recommending timers, visual clocks, and accountability structures as evidence-informed coping strategies.

  - **Supported.** Matches ICD-11 inattention criteria directly: "having difficulty planning, managing and organizing schoolwork, tasks and other activities" and general sustained-attention difficulties.

- **Claim 3:** For children with dyslexia, reading and decoding are common obstacles

## Video 56 — What you should know about ADHD medication

### Transcript

```text
Three things to know about ADHD medication. One, ADHD medication is not a cure-all. It can help alleviate your ADHD symptoms, but it won't magically cure all your problems, like getting organized or planning things. Two, there are two kinds of ADHD meds, stimulants and non-stimulants, and they can have different side effects. ```
```

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `343,191 5,918 94 29`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD medication is not a "cure-all" — it can help alleviate symptoms but won't magically fix everything (e.g., getting organized, planning)
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's framework, where medication is one component of a broader "comprehensive, holistic shared treatment plan" (1.5.2) that also includes psychological, behavioral, and educational/occupational support; NICE never frames medication as a total solution, and non-pharmacological interventions like CBT are recommended alongside medication precisely because symptoms like organization/planning often persist (1.5.14, 1.5.17).

- **Claim 2:** There are two kinds of ADHD medications — stimulants and non-stimulants — and they can have different side effects
  - **Supported.** Directly matches NICE's medication framework, which distinguishes stimulants (methylphenidate, lisdexamfetamine, dexamfetamine) from non-stimulants (atomoxetine, guanfacine) (1.7.7–1.7.15), and documents differing associated adverse effects (e.g., atomoxetine's specific link to sexual dysfunction in 1.8.15, versus cardiovascular/appetite effects more broadly discussed for stimulants).

- **Claim 3:** Research shows that properly treating ADHD with medication appears to reduce the risk of substance abuse, not increase it
  - **Supported.** Confirmed by multiple independent, large-scale studies, including a 146-million-person U.S. dataset analysis published in the American Journal of Psychiatry finding a 35% reduction in substance-abuse-related ER visits for men and 31% for women among ADHD patients using medication, and a corroborating Swedish national registry study with similar findings.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **3**
- Unsupported claims: **0**
- Supported claims: **3**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(3 − 0) / 3 × 100 = 100.0%**
- **Corrected label: Label 1 — Accurate**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD doesn't look the same in everyone — some present as the classic "can't sit still, blurting things out" type, others present quietly, anxiously, or perfectionistically while masking
  - **Unsupported.** ADHD presentations vary and some women mask symptoms, but anxiety and perfectionism are not defining ADHD presentations; the compound claim blurs association and diagnosis.

- **Claim 2:** People often think ADHD is "fake" because they only recognize one stereotype (the hyperactive young boy), when ADHD in adults can present differently
  - **Supported.** ICD-11 does support that presentation varies with age (hyperactivity often decreasing and being reported instead as internal restlessness in adolescents/adults) and that ADHD is more prevalent among males with females more likely to show inattentive symptoms — this lends some support to the idea that a narrow stereotype misses other presentations; however, the specific claim that skepticism about ADHD's legitimacy stems primarily from stereotype-narrowness (rather than other factors) is an interpretive claim not directly addressed in the reference materials.

- **Claim 3:** In adults and trauma survivors, ADHD often "hides behind" burnout, forgetfulness, emotional overwhelm, and executive dysfunction
  - **Unsupported.** Forgetfulness and executive dysfunction are supported, but trauma, burnout, and emotional overwhelm do not constitute a hidden ADHD presentation as a general rule.

- **Claim 4:** This isn't about people faking ADHD for attention or medication — it's about wider recognition of how ADHD can look different, especially in those missed/dismissed/misdiagnosed for years
  - **Unsupported.** Broader recognition is real, but the categorical claim that apparent cases are not about faking/medication seeking cannot be established for all individuals.

  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11 inattention criteria around difficulty with tasks requiring sustained mental effort, and NICE's acknowledgment that ADHD symptoms affect functioning across work, education, and relationships; the "knowing but not doing" pattern is a reasonable characterization of these difficulties.

- **Claim 2:** Being late to important events (e.g., job interviews) despite knowing their importance
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's inattention/time-management criteria (difficulty planning/organizing tasks) and the additional clinical features noting ADHD can significantly affect occupational functioning.

- **Claim 3:** According to Dr. William Dodson, neurotypical people are motivated by importance, rewards, and consequences, while people with ADHD are not motivated by these at all
  - **Unsupported.** The video accurately attributes this framework to William Dodson, but importance, rewards, and consequences do motivate many people with ADHD; the absolute group split is not a validated scientific model.

- **Claim 4:** Instead, people with ADHD are motivated by five separate factors: interest, competition/challenge, urgency, novelty/creativity, and passion
  - **Unsupported.** The ICNU/INCUP list is a popular clinical heuristic, not an established exhaustive set of the only factors that motivate ADHD brains.

  - **Unsupported.** "rejection sensitive dysphoria" is not a recognized diagnostic term or listed feature in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11; it is a popular clinical-practice concept (most associated with Dr. William Dodson) but is not part of the official diagnostic criteria or associated features described in the attached reference materials.

  - **Unsupported.** Emotional escalation can occur in many conditions and relationships; the proposed universal conflict chain is not ADHD-specific evidence.

- **Claim 4:** Strategies like taking a step back, deep breathing, counting to five, and seeing the broader picture can help regulate emotions before escalating

I imagine if you've you have ADHD and you've spent a lot of time trying to do different things and have gotten frustrated and felt like nothing's working, those thoughts might have popped into your head. So next time you're getting frustrated thinking nothing works, you know, I can't do this. Why should I even try or why should I listen to anyone that has all these strategies? - **Claim 2:** Trying quick-tip strategies from videos/articles without success doesn't necessarily mean the strategies are wrong — it may mean they weren't the right fit for that individual
  - **Supported.** Aligns with the individualized-treatment principle throughout NICE (e.g., 1.7.3's emphasis on tailoring treatment to individual needs, and 1.7.21's note that medication effect size and adverse effects "vary from person to person," which reflects the broader principle that ADHD interventions are not uniformly effective across individuals).

- **Claim 3:** Success often depends not just on the strategies themselves, but on finding ones that fit the individual and sometimes working with someone (e.g., a professional) to implement them and learn a different process
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's recommendation of structured psychological interventions and follow-up support (e.g., 1.5.18's "structured supportive psychological intervention focused on ADHD" with "regular follow-up") as part of implementing behavioral approaches effectively, rather than expecting self-directed strategies alone to suffice.

- **Claim 4:** If something isn't working, repeating the same approach over and over is unlikely to lead to improvement — reevaluating and adjusting the approach (not just the strategy) can make a meaningful difference
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's ongoing review and adjustment framework for ADHD management (e.g., 1.10.1's requirement for regular comprehensive review of "whether medication has been optimised" and treatment effectiveness, reflecting the broader principle that ineffective approaches should be reassessed and adjusted rather than persisted with unchanged).

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Some amount of sensory input is necessary for some brains to maintain focus
  - **Supported.** Matches the well-documented "optimal stimulation theory"/"moderate brain arousal model" research base, confirmed across multiple peer-reviewed studies showing ADHD brains often operate in an under-aroused state that benefits from added sensory input.

- **Claim 2:** Studies show that people with ADHD find it easier to maintain focus when there's white noise or music in the background
  - **Supported.** Confirmed by multiple peer-reviewed studies (e.g., a 2007 Journal of Child Psychology and Psychiatry study, replicated in 2010; a 2022 pilot study on verbal working memory; a 2024 Communications Biology/Nature study on modulated music improving sustained attention), though it's worth noting effects aren't universal — one cited 2024 study found about a third of ADHD participants performed worse with white noise, so individual variation exists.

- **Claim 3:** Some people have difficulty meditating with eyes fully closed, and some traditions recommend a "half-lidded gaze" allowing some sensory input during meditation
  - **Excluded from scoring.** This is a general meditation-practice claim rather than an ADHD medical claim, so it is excluded from scoring.

- **Claim 4:** If the brain is deprived of sensory input, it will create its own sensory input, and people with ADHD will self-stimulate ("fidget"/"widdle") if forced to sit still without any stimulus
  - **Unsupported.** Fidgeting can be an ADHD symptom, but the claim that sensory deprivation forces the ADHD brain to manufacture stimulation is an unverified universal mechanism.

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `1,319 98 3 84`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD might not be the real problem — it might actually be something happening in your sleep (specifically sleep apnea), which doctors often aren't checking for
  - **Unsupported.** Obstructive sleep apnea can mimic or worsen attention problems and should be considered, but the title broadly substitutes it for ADHD and adds an unquantified claim that doctors often miss it.

- **Claim 2:** There is similarity between brain scans in ADHD and sleep apnea
  - **Unsupported.** Both conditions have group-level neuroimaging findings, but that does not establish that their ‘brain scans are similar’ in a diagnostically meaningful way.

- **Claim 3:** Sleep apnea produces the same deficits in attention and reward circuitry as ADHD
  - **Unsupported.** Attention problems can overlap; evidence does not support the stronger claim that sleep apnea produces the same reward-circuit deficits as ADHD.

- **Claim 4:** A "gold standard" meta-analysis has shown that treating sleep apnea will reverse a lot of these same ADHD-like issues
  - **Unsupported.** Treating pediatric sleep apnea can improve ADHD-like symptoms, but the claimed ‘gold standard’ reversal result, especially generalized to adults, overstates the evidence.

- **Claim 5:** Recommends talking to a doctor about diagnostic workup if you suspect ADHD but have poor sleep, tiredness, or morning dry mouth
  - **Supported.** Consistent with the broader literature on differentiating ADHD from sleep-related causes of attention problems, and appropriately cautious in recommending professional evaluation rather than self-diagnosis.

- **Claim 6:** Sleeping on your side, elevating the head of the bed, inclining the whole bed, and avoiding eating within 3-4 hours of bed can help reduce sleep apnea episodes
  - **Unsupported.** Side-sleeping and head elevation can help selected obstructive sleep apnea cases, but the combined claim adds a 3–4-hour food rule not established as an apnea-reduction intervention.

- **Claim 7:** Breathing exercises and tongue exercises can help reduce sleep apnea episodes
  - **Supported.** Confirmed by multiple studies: tongue training reduced OSA events by up to 48% in one 2015 study, and myofunctional/oropharyngeal exercise RCTs showed AHI reductions of roughly 35-50%.

- **Claim 8:** These interventions can reduce apnea episodes by roughly 10-30% each
  - **Unsupported.** Effects differ by intervention and patient; research does not support a general 10–30% reduction for each listed measure.

- **Claim 9:** Boosting nitric oxide may help with oxygen flow relevant to sleep apnea
  - **Unsupported.** Nitric-oxide biology has been studied in sleep apnea, but ‘boosting nitric oxide’ is not an established treatment recommendation.

### Corrected result

- Unsupported claims: **7**
- Supported claims: **2**

- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** "Can Sugar or Screens Cause ADHD?" — implicitly and then explicitly answered as no
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's classification of ADHD as a neurodevelopmental disorder rooted in a persistent, cross-situational, childhood-onset pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity, not attributable to diet or environmental exposure like screen time; this is also consistent with NICE's rejection of dietary factors as a general cause or cure for ADHD (1.6.1–1.6.5), including its explicit statement not to advise elimination of specific dietary components as a generally applicable treatment, reflecting the absence of an evidence base for sugar/diet as causally producing ADHD.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD is "a difference in the brain"
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ADHD's classification as a neurodevelopmental disorder in ICD-11, which frames the condition as rooted in atypical neurodevelopment rather than external/environmental causation.

- **Claim 3:** Eating sugar will not create ADHD, though it may cause "a whole lot of other things"
  - **Supported.** Consistent with NICE's dietary guidance, which does not identify sugar as a cause of ADHD; NICE 1.6.2 does explicitly reject eliminating artificial colorings/additives as a general ADHD treatment (implying these dietary factors are not established causal agents), and no attached reference identifies sugar intake as a cause of the disorder.

- **Claim 4:** Watching enough phone/screen time will not create ADHD
  - **Supported.** Consistent with ICD-11's essential features, which require childhood-onset, persistent, cross-situational symptoms as the basis for diagnosis; no attached reference identifies screen exposure as a causal mechanism for ADHD.

- **Claim 5:** If you have ADHD, you will likely be more attracted to sugar and screens than neurotypical individuals, because of the dopamine highs they provide
  - **Unsupported.** ADHD is associated with reward sensitivity and problematic digital-media use, but the combined sugar/screen attraction claim and ‘dopamine highs’ mechanism are overgeneralized and do not establish a universal preference.

- **Claim 1:** The idea that "everybody has a little bit of ADHD" is misleading — using an analogy that everyone urinates, but urinating 60 times a day indicates something is genuinely wrong
  - **Supported.** This analogy captures the substance of ICD-11's "boundary with normality (threshold)" section, which explicitly states that inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity symptoms are present in many people to some degree, but a diagnosis requires these symptoms to be "persistent across time, pervasive across situations and significantly out of keeping with developmental level" — i.e., it's a matter of degree/frequency crossing a clinical threshold, not simply the presence or absence of the symptom.

- **Claim 2:** There's a difference between a neurotypical person feeling overwhelmed occasionally in objectively overwhelming situations (e.g., a noisy, crowded place) versus experiencing that overwhelm repeatedly, day in and day out, multiple times a day
  - **Supported.** This directly reflects the same ICD-11 "boundary with normality" principle: symptoms/reactions common to everyone become diagnostically significant when they are persistent and pervasive across situations and time, rather than occasional and context-appropriate.

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** Adderall is considered a Schedule II controlled substance, meaning it has a high potential for abuse and dependence
  - **Supported.** Confirmed by multiple sources: Adderall is explicitly classified as a Schedule II stimulant under the U.S. Controlled Substances Act, alongside other high-abuse-potential drugs.

- **Claim 2:** At the pharmacy you'll typically only get a month's supply at a time, and can usually fill it 2 days early to prevent stockpiling, though insurance sometimes allows filling about a week early
  - **Unsupported.** U.S. federal law prohibits refills of Schedule II prescriptions but can permit multiple prescriptions totaling up to a 90-day supply; day-supply and early-fill rules vary by prescriber, state, pharmacy, and insurer, so the stated timing is not universal.

- **Claim 3:** Adderall is a first-line option for ADHD treatment
  - **Supported.** This reflects general U.S. clinical practice for stimulants as first-line ADHD treatment, though the attached NICE guideline (UK-based) specifically recommends methylphenidate as first-line for children/young people and lisdexamfetamine or methylphenidate (not Adderall specifically) as first-line for adults — Adderall's first-line status is accurate to broader U.S. clinical convention but isn't the specific first-line agent named in the attached NICE reference.

- **Claim 4:** Take Adderall in the morning because it may keep you up at night
  - **Supported.** Consistent with the well-established pharmacological profile of stimulant medications and standard prescribing practice to avoid sleep disruption; this aligns with NICE's own monitoring guidance around sleep pattern changes with stimulant medication (1.8.17).

- **Claim 5:** Initial dose is usually 5mg once daily for immediate release, and 10-20mg for extended release in adults
  - **Unsupported.** Official dosing differs by formulation, age, indication, and patient; the unqualified IR/XR starting-dose ranges do not accurately state one universal adult starting regimen.

- **Claim 6:** AAP guidelines don't recommend dextroamphetamine in patients ≤5 years old
  - **Supported.** AAP guidance for ages 4–5 recommends parent/teacher behavior therapy first and says dextroamphetamine cannot be recommended as initial medication because evidence is insufficient, despite historical FDA approval.

- **Claim 7:** Don't take Adderall with acidic foods or vitamin C because it can decrease absorption
  - **Supported.** Strongly confirmed by multiple independent pharmacological sources: vitamin C and acidic foods lower gut/urinary pH, reducing amphetamine absorption and increasing excretion, with one pharmacokinetic study cited showing roughly a 30% reduction in amphetamine exposure with vitamin C co-administration.

- **Claim 8:** There is no tapering needed when stopping the medication if you're taking the appropriate dose
  - **Unsupported.** Abrupt cessation after prolonged/high-dose amphetamine exposure can cause withdrawal symptoms, and medication changes should be clinician-guided; ‘no tapering needed’ is too absolute.

- **Claim 9:** Adderall can cause decreased appetite, so having a larger breakfast is recommended to help prevent weight loss
  - **Supported.** Directly matches NICE's guidance on managing weight loss as an adverse effect (1.8.6), which explicitly recommends "taking additional meals or snacks early in the morning... when stimulant effects have worn off" as a strategy — a larger breakfast (before the appetite-suppressing effects peak) is a reasonable, consistent application of this principle.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **9**
- Unsupported claims: **3**
- Supported claims: **6**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(9 − 3) / 9 × 100 = 66.7%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** ADHD is a brain-related developmental disorder
  - **Supported.** ADHD is classified by the DSM-5-TR as a neurodevelopmental disorder and by the ICD-11 as a neurodevelopmental disorder. The informal phrase “brain problem” is broadly consistent with this classification, provided it is not interpreted as meaning that ADHD can be diagnosed through brain imaging.

- **Claim 2:** The exact causes of ADHD remain unknown
  - **Supported.** No single exact cause of ADHD has been established; current evidence identifies multiple interacting genetic, neurobiological, and environmental risk factors.

- **Claim 3:** ADHD is multifactorial in nature
  - **Supported.** This accurately reflects the evidence that ADHD has a complex and heterogeneous aetiology involving numerous genetic and environmental influences rather than one universal cause.

- **Claim 4:** The prefrontal cortex is the last part of the brain to mature
  - **Supported.** The prefrontal cortex is widely described as one of the last brain regions to mature, but calling it definitively “the last” is imprecise; this minor overstatement does not materially alter the underlying point.

- **Claim 5:** People with ADHD consistently have reduced grey-matter thickness in the prefrontal cortex
  - **Unsupported.** Group-level structural differences have been reported in ADHD, including findings involving frontal regions, but the evidence does not establish reduced prefrontal cortical thickness as a consistent feature of all people with ADHD; findings vary by brain region, age, study design, and measurement, making the statement an overgeneralization.

- **Claim 6:** The ability to think exists because of grey matter
  - **Unsupported.** Grey matter contributes importantly to information processing and cognition, but thinking depends on interactions among grey matter, white-matter connections, and distributed neural networks; attributing thought solely to grey matter materially oversimplifies brain function.

- **Claim 7:** Prefrontal or cortical maturation is delayed in people with ADHD
  - **Supported.** Longitudinal research has reported delayed cortical maturation in children with ADHD, particularly in prefrontal regions, but this is a group-level research finding rather than a universal characteristic or diagnostic marker present in every person with ADHD.

- **Claim 8:** Myelination is delayed in people with ADHD
  - **Unsupported.** Research has identified white-matter differences associated with ADHD, but white-matter differences do not by themselves establish delayed myelination; the claim presents a specific developmental mechanism more conclusively than the available evidence supports.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **8**
- Unsupported claims: **3**
- Supported claims: **5**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(8 − 3) / 8 × 100 = 62.5%**
- **Corrected label: Label 2 — Slightly misleading**

**Metadata as supplied (views, likes, comments, duration):** `37,511 2,644 29 51`

### Corrected claims and fact-check

- **Claim 1:** In neurotypical brains, dopamine rises when a person begins a task, remains steady because the brain tracks the eventual reward, and gently decreases after completion
  - **Unsupported.** Dopamine signalling contributes to reward prediction and motivation, but the described universal “dopamine curve” is not an established model of how dopamine behaves throughout ordinary tasks. Dopamine activity varies according to reward prediction, salience, uncertainty, task conditions, and brain region; it cannot be represented reliably by this simple curve for all neurotypical people. Review of dopamine and motivational control.

- **Claim 2:** ADHD brains generally begin from a much lower baseline dopamine level than neurotypical brains
  - **Unsupported.** Altered dopaminergic signalling is implicated in ADHD, but current evidence does not establish that people with ADHD universally have a substantially lower measurable “baseline dopamine” level. The dopamine hypothesis of ADHD remains more complex and uncertain than a general dopamine deficiency. Critical review of the dopamine hypothesis of ADHD.

- **Claim 3:** Difficulty initiating tasks in ADHD is substantially caused by insufficient dopamine activation preventing the brain from crossing a neurological “start line”
  - **Unsupported.** People with ADHD can experience executive-function and motivational difficulties that interfere with initiating tasks, but the proposed dopamine threshold or “start line” is an explanatory metaphor, not an established neurological mechanism. Evidence connecting dopamine-related reward dysfunction with motivation does not demonstrate this specific causal process. ADHD dopamine-pathway study.

- **Claim 4:** Interest can make it easier for a person with ADHD to begin a task because interest increases dopamine enough to overcome the proposed start threshold
  - **Unsupported.** Interesting or immediately rewarding tasks may improve motivation and engagement in some people with ADHD, and atypical reward processing is supported by research. However, the claim that interest works specifically by raising dopamine above a required task-initiation threshold is not established and presents a proposed mechanism as fact.

- **Claim 5:** Urgency makes people with ADHD suddenly productive because it increases adrenaline, which combines with dopamine and enables them to cross the task-initiation threshold
  - **Unsupported.** Urgency or an approaching deadline may alter arousal and performance for some individuals, but there is insufficient evidence for the specific universal mechanism described here. The claim that adrenaline “pairs with dopamine” to cross an ADHD start threshold is an oversimplified neurochemical explanation rather than an established finding.

- **Claim 6:** When a person with ADHD cannot begin a task, this does not necessarily mean that they do not want to perform it
  - **Supported.** Executive and motivational impairment can block task initiation despite genuine intention, so failure to start should not automatically be read as unwillingness.

### Corrected result

- Fact-checkable claims: **6**
- Unsupported claims: **5**
- Supported claims: **1**
- Supported-claim percentage: **(6 − 5) / 6 × 100 = 16.7%**
- **Corrected label: Label 3 — Moderately misleading**

## Video 71

**Title:** ADHD Isn’t a Disease — It’s a Childhood Adaptation | Gabor Maté Explains  
**URL:** https://www.youtube.com/shorts/Y2xh25ZCBFE  
**Views:** 226,045  
**Likes:** 6,413  
**Comments:** 161

**Transcript:**

> I can tell you about my own ADHD. And this is where we go back to childhood again. My mother didn't abuse me. She did her best to look after me, but she was stressed, depressed, terrorized, grief-stricken. I'm picking that up as a sensitive infant. Can I fight back, change the situation, or escape? No, none of those. What can I do? Nothing I can do. My brain will tune out as a way of dealing with the stress. So that tuning out, that absent-mindedness, that desire to scatter your attention all over the place, that's not a disease. They say it's an inherited disease. The hell it is. It begins as a coping mechanism which then gets programmed into the brain. And as a lot of these early coping mechanisms function, they help you in the short term, create problems in the long term. And ADD is one of these examples.

### Claims identified & fact-check

**Claim 1:** ADHD begins as a coping mechanism in which a child’s brain tunes out in response to early stress, and this mechanism becomes programmed into the brain  
→ **Unsupported** — early adversity and stress are associated with ADHD and may influence symptom severity, but current evidence does not establish this as the general origin of ADHD. ADHD has a predominantly genetic, multifactorial aetiology, and environmental associations should not be converted into a universal trauma-based causal mechanism.

**Claim 2:** ADHD is not inherited  
→ **Unsupported** — ADHD is highly heritable and has a predominantly genetic aetiology involving many common and rare genetic variants. It is not inherited through a single gene in every case, but denying inherited influence contradicts strong evidence.

**Claim 3:** ADHD-related absent-mindedness and scattered attention are not manifestations of a neurodevelopmental disorder  
→ **Unsupported** — ADHD is formally classified as a neurodevelopmental disorder. Describing it as a coping response does not replace the diagnostic requirement that persistent inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity cause clinically significant impairment.

Claims identified & fact-check:

Claim 1: Approximately 15.5 million American adults have been diagnosed with ADHD
→ Supported — a 2023 nationally representative survey estimated that 15.5 million US adults, or 6%, reported a current ADHD diagnosis. CDC report

Claim 2: ADHD is not merely a problem with focusing
→ Supported — ADHD may involve inattention, hyperactivity, impulsivity, organization problems, forgetfulness, and functional impairment. Not everyone exhibits every feature

Claim 3: ADHD is highly genetic
→ Supported — the attached DSM-5-TR describes ADHD as substantially heritable, although environmental and developmental factors also contribute

Claim 4: Excessive talking can be an early ADHD sign that people overlook
→ Supported with qualification — excessive talking is a recognized hyperactive-impulsive symptom. However, it is nonspecific and cannot establish ADHD without the required persistent, impairing, cross-situational pattern

Claim 5: ADHD is often mistaken for anxiety or depression
→ Supported with qualification — anxiety and depression can produce restlessness or concentration problems resembling ADHD, and both can also coexist with ADHD. “Often” is not quantified, and comprehensive assessment is required to distinguish them

Claim 6: ADHD can affect relationships
→ Supported — impulsivity, inattention, forgetfulness, and organizational difficulties can impair social and family functioning, although the effects vary substantially

Instead of targeting just dopamine and norepinephrine, this new medication is targeting three neurotransmitters. It’s Monday, and you know what time it is. It’s time for drug of the day. So today’s medication that we’re going to talk about is serdexmethylphenidate, which is the generic for Azstarys. This just got FDA approved, so please, if I’m saying this wrong, don’t judge me. This is the first FDA-approved medication in a brand-new class of medications called NDSRI, which stands for norepinephrine, dopamine, serotonin reuptake inhibitor. Try saying that five times fast. This medication can be used for ADHD in adults as well as children who are six years of age and weigh at least 20 kg. The starting dose for adults is 210 mg by mouth every morning, and you can increase it to a maximum of 280 mg per day. How this medication works is it’s going to increase norepinephrine, so you’re going to have more alertness and be more attentive. It’s also going to increase dopamine, which is really great for motivation and reward processing. Then there’s serotonin, which honestly isn’t well documented for its purpose in ADHD, but it’s believed that it will probably help with impulsivity and controlling behaviours and emotions. When it comes to side effects, there will be decreased appetite, insomnia, dry mouth, and nausea. If you’re wondering if it will be a controlled substance like Adderall or Vyvanse, we actually don’t know yet because it hasn’t been classified by the DEA, but most likely it will probably be a controlled substance.

### Claims identified & fact-check

**Claim 1:** Centanafadine (Simtriyo) is a newly FDA-approved stimulant medication for ADHD  
→ **Supported** — the FDA approved Simtriyo (centanafadine) on 24 July 2026 for ADHD. The prescribing information describes it as a central nervous system stimulant.

**Claim 2:** Centanafadine inhibits the reuptake of norepinephrine, dopamine, and serotonin and belongs to the NDSRI class  
→ **Supported** — the FDA prescribing information describes centanafadine as a norepinephrine-dopamine-serotonin reuptake inhibitor with inhibitory activity at all three transporters.

**Claim 3:** The medication being discussed is serdexmethylphenidate, the generic drug in Azstarys  
→ **Unsupported** — this identifies a different medication. Simtriyo contains centanafadine, whereas Azstarys contains serdexmethylphenidate and dexmethylphenidate. Azstarys was FDA-approved in 2021, not newly approved in July 2026.

**Claim 4:** Simtriyo is indicated for adults and for children aged six or older who weigh at least 20 kg  
→ **Supported** — this matches the FDA indication.

**Claim 5:** The recommended adult starting dose is 210 mg each morning and may be increased to a maximum of 280 mg daily  
→ **Supported** — this matches the FDA adult dosing instructions.

**Claim 6:** Increasing norepinephrine produces alertness and attention, increasing dopamine improves motivation and reward processing, and increasing serotonin probably improves impulsivity and emotional control  
→ **Unsupported** — centanafadine acts at all three reuptake transporters, but the FDA states that its mechanism of action in ADHD remains unclear. The video presents simplified, transmitter-by-transmitter clinical effects—especially the proposed serotonin effects—as though they were established.

**Claim 7:** Decreased appetite, insomnia, nausea, and dry mouth are side effects of Simtriyo  
→ **Supported with qualification** — all four were reported in trials and are among the common adverse reactions in adults, although the most common reactions differ by age group.

**Claim 8:** At the time of the video, Simtriyo’s federal controlled-substance schedule had not yet been assigned, although scheduling was expected  
→ **Supported** — the initial FDA label stated that the controlled-substance schedule was pending and warned of abuse, misuse, and addiction risks.

Claims identified & fact-check:

Claim 1: Adderall and Ritalin should be used only when prescribed by an appropriate clinician
→ Supported — these are prescription-controlled stimulants and should not be taken without medical authorization and supervision

Claim 2: People without ADHD, particularly college students, sometimes use prescription stimulants nonmedically to study
→ Supported — nonmedical use among students is well documented, with academic performance commonly reported as a motive. This does not mean that most college students misuse them. College-student study

Claim 3: Most ADHD medications are stimulants
→ Supported with qualification — stimulant medications are the most established and commonly used pharmacological treatments, but approved non-stimulants are also available

Claim 4: ADHD stimulants activate the central nervous system within 15–20 minutes of taking a pill
→ Unsupported — onset varies considerably according to the drug, formulation, dose, route, and individual. Immediate-release and extended-release products do not share a universal 15–20-minute onset

Claim 5: Caffeine is a stimulant, but prescription ADHD stimulants generally exert stronger therapeutic central-nervous-system effects
→ Supported with qualification — all are stimulants, but “more powerful” is imprecise because effects depend on dose and the biological effect being compared

Claim 6: Adderall is a commonly prescribed stimulant containing amphetamine salts
→ Supported — Adderall contains mixed amphetamine salts and is prescribed for ADHD

Claim 7: Using or possessing Adderall without a prescription is unlawful in the United States
→ Supported — amphetamine products are Schedule II controlled substances. Exact legal consequences depend on jurisdiction and circumstances

Claim 8: Possible stimulant adverse effects include headache, dry mouth, elevated blood pressure or heart rate, insomnia, weight loss, irritability, libido changes, and erectile dysfunction
→ Supported with qualification — these are recognized possible adverse effects, but their frequency differs and an individual will not necessarily experience them all. Adderall XR prescribing information

Claims identified & fact-check:

Claim 1: ADHD and OCD can coexist
→ Supported with qualification — the comorbidity is clinically recognized, but “all the time” substantially exaggerates its frequency

Claim 2: Co-occurring ADHD and OCD occurs particularly among the children and grandchildren of people with alcoholism
→ Unsupported — family histories may contain shared genetic and environmental risks, but this specific intergenerational pattern is not an established characteristic of ADHD–OCD comorbidity

Claim 3: Co-occurring ADHD and OCD is explained by an overactive middle-frontal brain region, an underactive inferior orbital prefrontal cortex, and low serotonin and dopamine
→ Unsupported — neither condition, nor their coexistence, can be reduced to this scan pattern or diagnosed as a simple dual-neurotransmitter deficiency

Claim 4: 5-HTP, rhodiola, ashwagandha, and ginseng balance the brain and treat co-occurring ADHD and OCD
→ Unsupported — these supplements are not established treatments for this comorbidity. Standard management addresses each disorder individually and may include ADHD medication, OCD-focused CBT with exposure and response prevention, and serotonergic medication under clinical supervision. Clinical review

The speaker’s report that Prozac and Ritalin were “miraculous for some patients” is a personal clinical anecdote and was excluded from scoring.

Claims identified & fact-check:

Claim 1: Researchers have added “fib” as an ADHD-specific fourth response alongside fight, flight, and freeze
→ Unsupported — “fib” is not a recognized threat response or ADHD construct in the attached DSM-5-TR, ICD-11, or clinical guidelines

Claim 2: People with ADHD, particularly children and teenagers, are characteristically known to lie
→ Unsupported — lying is not a diagnostic symptom or established defining feature of ADHD. Impulsivity, forgetfulness, incomplete task performance, or inconsistent recall should not automatically be interpreted as either lying or an ADHD-specific form of dishonesty

Claim 3: When people with ADHD lie, the usual cause is self-preservation rather than any other motivation
→ Unsupported — people may lie for many situational, developmental, interpersonal, or psychological reasons. No evidence establishes self-preservation as the usual ADHD-specific explanation

Claim 4: Every child with ADHD has received more than 20,000 corrective messages by age ten
→ Unsupported — the number originated as a hypothetical calculation assuming repeated school corrections, not as the measured result of a controlled study. The original article said a child “could” receive that number, whereas the video presents it as an established universal statistic. Original article

Claim 5: People with ADHD lie because panic prevents them from processing the facts or determining what answer another person wants
→ Unsupported — panic can affect recall or communication in anyone, but this is not an established ADHD mechanism and “determining what story you are looking for” describes tailoring an answer rather than a recognized symptom

Claim 6: An ADHD person’s immediate impulsive reaction is usually to lie as a form of protection from punishment or damaged self-esteem
→ Unsupported — impulsivity can lead to speaking without adequate deliberation, but evidence does not establish lying as the usual impulsive response. Avoiding punishment is also not specific to ADHD

Claims identified & fact-check:

Claim 1: ADHD can coexist with autism
→ Supported — autism and ADHD are separate diagnoses that frequently coexist. One US study found that approximately one in eight children diagnosed with ADHD also had autism. CDC-hosted study

Claim 2: ADHD is a separate condition that can be treated with medication
→ Supported with qualification — medication is an established ADHD treatment, but behavioural, psychological, educational, and environmental interventions may also be appropriate

Claim 3: Down syndrome is caused by a chromosomal abnormality
→ Supported — it generally results from an extra copy of chromosome 21. CDC Down syndrome overview

Claim 4: Down syndrome affects every bodily system
→ Unsupported as stated — Down syndrome can affect multiple organ systems, but manifestations vary between individuals and it is too absolute to say every system is affected in every person

Claim 5: People with Down syndrome may have intellectual disability or congenital heart problems
→ Supported — developmental or intellectual disability is common, and congenital heart defects occur at increased rates

Claim 6: ADHD, autism, learning disabilities, and Down syndrome are separate conditions
→ Supported with qualification — they have different diagnostic and biological definitions, although they can coexist. “Learning disabilities” describes a heterogeneous group rather than one condition

The isolated phrase “hyperactive child … only therapy” was too incomplete to identify a reliable proposition and was excluded from scoring.

→ Supported with qualification — rejection sensitivity and emotional dysregulation have been reported among people with ADHD, but “rejection sensitive dysphoria” is not a DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 diagnosis or core ADHD symptom, and the evidence remains developing

Claim 2: Creativity, problem-solving ability, curiosity, and excitement are inherent ADHD “superpowers”
→ Unsupported — individuals with ADHD may possess these strengths, but they are not established universal or defining consequences of ADHD

Claim 3: ADHD can involve painful and debilitating impairment
→ Supported — clinically significant impairment is central to diagnosis, although its nature and severity differ between individuals

Claim 4: The painful or debilitating parts of ADHD are unchangeable
→ Unsupported — ADHD can persist, but symptoms and impairment can change with development, treatment, accommodations, environment, and learned strategies

## Video 80

**Title:** ADHD + Autism = AuDHD. Here's what that can really feel like…  
**URL:** https://www.youtube.com/shorts/wJT9vX_zDck  
**Views:** 153,238  
**Likes:** 6,153  
**Comments:** 174  
**Duration:** 58 seconds

**Transcript:**

> ADHD and autism frequently co-occur. Many people with one or the two diagnoses show elevated traits of both ADHD and autism. Common experiences for ADHD and autism include sensory differences, intense focus on specific interests, rejection sensitivity, executive dysfunction, sleep issues, and emotional regulation. Someone with ADHD is more likely to seek out novelty and make more impulsive decisions, whereas an autistic person is more likely to crave routine, structure, and order. If someone is autistic and has ADHD, they’re known as AuDHD. They may experience an internal struggle and tension between their competing autistic and ADHD traits and a heightened experience of shared traits.

### Claims identified & fact-check

**Claim 1:** ADHD and autism frequently co-occur  
→ **Supported** — co-diagnosis is well established. Reviews report substantial ADHD prevalence among autistic children and elevated autism prevalence among children with ADHD.

**Claim 2:** People diagnosed with either condition may show elevated traits associated with the other  
→ **Supported** — ADHD and autism have distinct diagnostic criteria but show meaningful phenotypic overlap, and clinically significant traits of the other condition may occur without necessarily satisfying all criteria for a second diagnosis.

**Claim 3:** Sensory differences, executive dysfunction, sleep problems, and emotional-regulation difficulties can occur in both ADHD and autism  
→ **Supported** — these features are documented across both conditions, although none is present in every affected person and some are not core diagnostic criteria.

**Claim 4:** Intense focus on particular interests can occur in both ADHD and autism  
→ **Supported with qualification** — restricted or highly focused interests are characteristic of autism, while intense task engagement or “hyperfocus” is reported in ADHD research and clinical accounts; the features are not necessarily identical in mechanism or presentation.

**Claim 5:** Rejection sensitivity is a common feature of both ADHD and autism  
→ **Unsupported** — distress following rejection can occur, but “rejection sensitivity” or “rejection-sensitive dysphoria” is not a formal diagnostic feature of either condition, and the evidence does not justify presenting it as a general shared characteristic.

**Claim 6:** People with ADHD are more likely to seek novelty and make impulsive decisions, whereas autistic people are more likely to prefer routine, structure, and order  
→ **Supported with qualification** — impulsivity is a core ADHD domain, and insistence on sameness, routines, or restricted patterns is central to autism. However, these are group-level tendencies and should not be treated as universal or mutually exclusive.

**Claim 7:** “AuDHD” is a term for a person who has both autism and ADHD  
→ **Supported with qualification** — AuDHD is a widely used informal community term for co-occurring autism and ADHD, but it is not a separate DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 diagnosis.

**Claim 8:** Co-occurring autism and ADHD can produce tension between apparently competing traits and a heightened burden from overlapping difficulties  
→ **Supported with qualification** — the “internal tension” wording describes subjective experience, but research supports greater functional impairment and often more severe difficulties in people with both conditions than in people with only one.

### Result

**Total claims:** 8  
**False/unsupported claims:** 1  
**Supported-claim percentage:** (8−1)/8 × 100 = 87.5%  
**Label:** 1 — Accurate

## Video 81

**Title:** 13 Ways ADHD Affects Eating Habits #adhd  
**URL:** https://www.youtube.com/shorts/WhUCpl7Ogg4  
**Views:** 7,252  
**Likes:** 530  
**Comments:** 16  
**Duration:** 175 seconds

**Transcript:**

> Here are 13 ways in which ADHD can affect your eating habits. Number one: people with ADHD often have really poor body proprioception, which means that knowing when you're hungry, knowing when you're full, and what that feels like can be really challenging. Number two: really common medications for ADHD, such as Adderall and Vyvanse, can really suppress appetite, which means that trying to rely on hunger cues alone can lead to undereating. Number three: time blindness and hyperfocus can make it really challenging to act on hunger cues when they do arise. Number four: people with ADHD often have a hard time prioritizing things appropriately, so if you notice that you are hungry but you are doing something that your brain says is more important, it can be really difficult to prioritize acting on hunger unless that cue is really intense. Number five: people with ADHD often do really well with routines and structure; however, they also have a really strong need for novelty, which makes meal planning and meal preparation challenging. One part of your brain wants to prepare everything so you do not have to think about it and can eat the same thing every day, while the other part cannot have the same thing every day. Number six: unless that food is a hyperfixation food, at which point you must eat the same thing every day until, suddenly and without warning, the sight of that food makes you want to be sick. Number seven: not getting enough fuel during the day because you are focused on other things, or because your appetite is poor, can affect focus and executive function, making it harder to choose more nourishing, substantial meals in the evening. Number eight: having nourishing things to eat during the day may sound simple, but it requires many steps, which can be overwhelming and exhausting, lead to task avoidance, and cause many people with ADHD to rely on convenience food or takeout. Number nine: cooking can be overstimulating for many people with ADHD because of the sounds, smells, and visual mess. Number ten: food is an accessible and reliable way to boost dopamine when you feel burnt out, which many people with ADHD chronically are, so snacking is probably not merely a lack of willpower or boredom but is meeting core needs. Number eleven: because food can reliably and easily boost dopamine, many people may use it to help with task initiation and completion. Number twelve: for some people with ADHD, food can be a stimming behaviour and help meet stimulation needs. Number thirteen: for many people, eating can feel like the most accessible activity when stuck in “waiting mode.” Because of these dynamics, binge eating is common in the ADHD community.

### Claims identified & fact-check

**Claim 1:** People with ADHD often have impaired awareness of hunger and fullness signals  
→ **Supported with qualification** — altered interoceptive processing has been reported in ADHD, but the evidence is not strong enough to say that people with ADHD generally have “really poor” body awareness. Hunger and satiety awareness varies substantially between individuals.

**Claim 2:** Common stimulant medications such as Adderall and Vyvanse can suppress appetite, and relying only on hunger cues may therefore contribute to undereating  
→ **Supported** — decreased appetite is a well-established adverse effect of amphetamine-based ADHD medication. A person whose medication suppresses appetite may not receive reliable hunger cues during the medication’s active period.

**Claim 3:** Time-management difficulties and prolonged absorption in an activity can make it difficult for a person with ADHD to notice or act on hunger cues  
→ **Supported with qualification** — ADHD-related inattention, poor time awareness, and difficulty shifting between activities can interfere with regular eating. “Time blindness” and “hyperfocus” are informal descriptions rather than diagnostic criteria.

**Claim 4:** ADHD-related executive-function and prioritization difficulties can cause a person to postpone eating until hunger becomes intense  
→ **Supported with qualification** — difficulties organizing, sequencing, initiating, and switching tasks can interfere with meal timing, although this is not experienced by every person with ADHD.

**Claim 5:** A combination of reliance on routine and desire for novelty can make meal planning and preparation difficult for people with ADHD  
→ **Supported with qualification** — routines can compensate for executive difficulties, while reward- and novelty-related tendencies may affect adherence to repetitive plans. The described internal conflict is a possible experience, not a universal ADHD mechanism.

**Claim 6:** People with ADHD characteristically eat one “hyperfixation food” repeatedly and then develop a sudden aversion to it  
→ **Unsupported** — this experience is widely described online, but it is not an established ADHD characteristic and is not supported well enough to generalize to people with ADHD.

**Claim 7:** Undereating can impair attention and executive performance and make later food decisions more difficult  
→ **Supported with qualification** — inadequate energy intake and hunger can impair cognitive performance. The precise effect and severity depend on the individual and do not uniquely arise from ADHD.

**Claim 8:** The multiple executive steps involved in obtaining and preparing food can overwhelm some people with ADHD and increase reliance on convenience food or takeout  
→ **Supported with qualification** — planning, sequencing, working-memory, and task-initiation difficulties can make multistep meal preparation harder. The claimed behavioural consequence is plausible and reported, but is not inevitable.

**Claim 9:** Sounds, smells, and visual clutter during cooking can be overstimulating for people with ADHD  
→ **Supported with qualification** — sensory-processing differences occur more often in people with ADHD than in comparison groups, so cooking may be overstimulating for some. Sensory over-responsivity is not a core ADHD diagnostic criterion.

**Claim 10:** Food reliably boosts dopamine in a way that explains ADHD-related snacking and shows that the behaviour is meeting core neurological needs  
→ **Unsupported** — food engages reward systems, but the video turns a complex interaction among appetite, reward, habits, emotions, environment, and medication into a simple dopamine-deficiency explanation. A person’s snacking cannot be assigned this cause without assessment.

**Claim 11:** People with ADHD use food to initiate and complete tasks specifically because food boosts dopamine  
→ **Unsupported** — eating may be used as a reward or source of stimulation, but the proposed dopamine mechanism and its general role in ADHD task initiation have not been established.

**Claim 12:** Eating is an ADHD “stimming” behaviour that meets stimulation needs  
→ **Unsupported** — repetitive eating can serve sensory or self-regulatory functions for some individuals, but it is not established as a characteristic ADHD behaviour. The statement lacks sufficient evidence as a general ADHD claim.

**Claim 13:** Binge eating is more common among people with ADHD  
→ **Supported** — systematic reviews and meta-analyses find a significant association between ADHD and binge-eating or other disordered-eating outcomes. This does not mean that most people with ADHD binge eat or that ADHD alone causes binge eating.

### Result

**Total claims:** 13  
**False/unsupported claims:** 4  
**Supported-claim percentage:** (13−4)/13 × 100 = 69.2%  
**Label:** 2 — Slightly misleading

## Video 82

**Title:** For ADHD, Motivation Is the Wrong Fuel  
**URL:** https://www.youtube.com/shorts/b8eHtXxUGQI  
**Views:** 968  
**Likes:** 20  
**Comments:** 0  
**Duration:** 28 seconds

**Transcript:**

> Stop waiting to feel motivated. Motivation does not work for an ADHD brain; it is the wrong fuel. Your ADHD brain works on urgency, importance, interest, novelty, and challenge, none of which are motivation. We therefore have to manufacture something to make a potentially boring task feel more urgent. This can mean telling someone it will be done by a deadline. It can also mean body doubling. Make the boring task urgent and your brain will switch on.

### Claims identified & fact-check

**Claim 1:** Motivation does not work for an ADHD brain and is the “wrong fuel”  
→ **Unsupported** — ADHD can affect motivation and reward processing, but people with ADHD are not incapable of being motivated. The statement replaces substantial individual variation with an absolute neurological rule.

**Claim 2:** ADHD brains operate on urgency, importance, interest, novelty, and challenge rather than motivation  
→ **Unsupported** — these factors may increase engagement for some people with ADHD, but this list is not an accepted diagnostic or neuroscientific model and the factors described are themselves forms or sources of motivation.

**Claim 3:** Creating urgency through a deadline or using body doubling will switch an ADHD brain on  
→ **Unsupported** — deadlines and body doubling may be useful practical strategies for some people, but the evidence does not support a guaranteed neurological “switch-on” effect. Effectiveness varies by person and task.

### Result

**Total claims:** 3  
**False/unsupported claims:** 3  
**Supported-claim percentage:** (3−3)/3 × 100 = 0%  
**Label:** 4 — Highly misleading

## Video 83

**Title:** What 70% of ADHD Diagnoses Get Wrong  
**URL:** https://www.youtube.com/shorts/PRouUk1Ge9I  
**Views:** 56  
**Likes:** 3  
**Comments:** 0  
**Duration:** 63 seconds

**Transcript:**

> The majority of people—truly, around 70%—who come in with an ADHD diagnosis, or who are self-proclaimed ADHD, do not have a neurological ADHD pattern at all. In our world, we look at something called a theta-beta ratio: how much theta production there is relative to beta. Most people think they have ADHD because they are hyperactive, cannot sit still, are easily bored, and so on. A true ADHD person is actually swimming in their own theta. They are in a slower, calmer, more creative, more relaxed state. You cannot be slow, tired, and lethargic during the day, so the brain constantly tries to put itself into an alert state. It produces all this little beta, but it is not sustainable. That is the classic pattern, which most people honestly do not have. Usually, they have a very fast processing speed and a lot of high beta, so it is hard to sit still, but it is a completely different pattern.

### Claims identified & fact-check

**Claim 1:** Around 70% of people presenting with an ADHD diagnosis or self-identifying as having ADHD do not have the real neurological ADHD pattern  
→ **Unsupported** — no source or representative diagnostic study supports this 70% figure. Self-identification and a formal diagnosis should also not be combined into one population when estimating misdiagnosis.

**Claim 2:** A theta-to-beta EEG ratio can identify whether a person has “true” ADHD  
→ **Unsupported** — theta-to-beta ratio is not sufficiently accurate or specific to diagnose ADHD or distinguish it reliably from other conditions. ADHD remains a clinical diagnosis based on symptoms, development, impairment, multiple settings, and differential assessment.

**Claim 3:** A true ADHD person has excessive theta activity and is characteristically slower, calmer, more creative, and more relaxed  
→ **Unsupported** — ADHD does not have one universal EEG pattern, and these personality or processing descriptions are not diagnostic criteria. They exclude many legitimate ADHD presentations.

**Claim 4:** The ADHD brain compensates for being slow and lethargic by producing small bursts of beta activity that cannot be sustained  
→ **Unsupported** — this proposed compensatory mechanism is not an established explanation of ADHD symptoms.

**Claim 5:** The elevated-theta pattern is the classic real ADHD pattern, while people with high beta and fast processing who cannot sit still have a completely different condition  
→ **Unsupported** — EEG research shows heterogeneous and overlapping patterns. Neither theta nor beta profiles define who truly has ADHD, and EEG cannot replace a proper diagnostic assessment.

### Result

**Total claims:** 5  
**False/unsupported claims:** 5  
**Supported-claim percentage:** (5−5)/5 × 100 = 0%  
**Label:** 4 — Highly misleading
